coordinator · Research baby-science facts for lyrics11:18 PM

You are researching primary scientific literature for a fact sheet that will feed a dense, fact-packed song about infant science (think Hamilton-style lyrical density). Deliverable: return your findings as your final message in Markdown (do NOT write any files). Use WebSearch and WebFetch (load them via ToolSearch "select:WebSearch,WebFetch" first).

Themes you own: SOCIAL/BONDING, MORALITY, NATURE-NURTURE/TEMPERAMENT, and a DEBUNKED/OVERSTATED CLAIMS section. For each fact, give: one or two lines, the specific number(s), the primary paper (first author, year, journal) or best authoritative source, a URL, and a reliability flag: [solid] / [contested] / [pop-science-only]. Prefer primary papers and university press releases over blogs. Verify numbers; do not invent them. If you cannot verify a number, say so explicitly.

Items to verify and quantify (add others you find striking):

SOCIAL / BONDING

  • Ruth Feldman (Reichman University / Bar-Ilan / Yale): oxytocin rises in mothers AND fathers across the first months (Gordon, Zagoory-Sharon, Leckman & Feldman 2010 Biological Psychiatry: 80 couples, fathers' oxytocin equal to mothers'); Abraham et al. 2014 PNAS: primary-caregiving gay fathers show amygdala activation like mothers plus superior temporal sulcus activation like fathers (48 couples; 89 parents); "bio-behavioral synchrony"; Feldman in Netflix Babies "Love" episode - confirm and describe what was filmed.
  • Ed Tronick (UMass Boston): still-face paradigm (Tronick, Als, Adamson, Wise & Brazelton 1978 J Am Acad Child Psychiatry): timeline (2 min play, 2 min still face, 2 min reunion); infants' response within seconds; "good enough" parenting - matched/mismatched states ~30% of the time (Tronick & Gianino 1986 - verify the "70% miscoordinated" / "repair" claim); Tronick in Netflix Babies "Love"? confirm.
  • Harlow's monkeys (cloth vs wire mother, 1958) - one line with number (hours per day on cloth mother ~17-18 h vs <1 h wire).
  • Attachment: Bowlby; Ainsworth Strange Situation 1978 (Baltimore; ~65% secure, 20% avoidant, 15% resistant in US samples; van IJzendoorn 1988 cross-cultural meta-analysis); separation anxiety onset ~8 months, stranger anxiety 6-12 months; disorganized attachment ~15%.
  • Charles Nelson / Nathan Fox / Charles Zeanah: Bucharest Early Intervention Project (136 children, randomized to foster care vs institution; children placed before ~24 months had IQ gains ~ 8-10 points; EEG; Nelson 2007 Science; Nelson, Fox & Zeanah "Romania's Abandoned Children" 2014). Was Nelson in Netflix "Babies" ("Relationships" or "Nature and Nurture")? confirm.
  • Skin-to-skin / kangaroo care: Charpak 2017 Pediatrics 20-year follow-up (Colombia), WHO 2021 immediate KMC trial (NEJM 2021: 25% lower mortality in low-birth-weight infants).
  • Social smile ~6-8 weeks; laughter ~3-4 months; social referencing ~10-12 months (Sorce, Emde, Campos & Klinnert 1985 visual cliff: 74% crossed when mother smiled, 0 when she looked fearful).
  • Joint attention / gaze following ~9-12 months; Tomasello's "shared intentionality"; Meltzoff's "like me".
  • Gaze / eye contact: newborns prefer direct gaze (Farroni 2002 PNAS); newborn and mother gaze synchrony.
  • Contagious crying in newborns (Sagi & Hoffman 1976; Simner 1971; Martin & Clark 1982 - newborns cry more to another newborn's cry than to their own).
  • Fathers: Feldman and others on paternal brain; Lamb; fathers' testosterone drops after birth (Gettler 2011 PNAS, Philippines, 624 men: testosterone fell ~26% morning/ 34% evening among new fathers).
  • Maternal brain changes: Hoekzema 2017 Nature Neuroscience gray matter reductions lasting 2 years.
  • Michael Tomasello & Felix Warneken: toddlers help spontaneously - Warneken & Tomasello 2006 Science (18-month-olds helped in 22 of 24? out-of-reach objects; 84% helped at least once; chimpanzees too); Warneken 2008: rewards undermine helping. Were they in Netflix Babies "Relationships" / "Toddlers"?
  • Dan Fessler (UCLA): anthropology - what infant work would he have featured in Netflix Babies? (Perhaps in "Nature and Nurture" or "Senses" on disgust / crying?) Confirm.
  • Peer play: parallel play ~2 years; toddlers share with peers.

MORALITY

  • Kiley Hamlin (UBC): Hamlin, Wynn & Bloom 2007 Nature "Social evaluation by preverbal infants" - 6- and 10-month-olds prefer the helper over the hinderer (puppet show; 14 of 16 10-month-olds and 12 of 12 6-month-olds? verify exact numbers); Hamlin 2011 PNAS 3-month-olds; Hamlin 2013 Psychological Science 8-month-olds prefer those who punish the hinderer; replication debate (Salvadori 2015; ManyBabies 4 2024 failed to replicate the helper preference - verify, give numbers). Was Hamlin in Netflix Babies "What Babies Know"? confirm.
  • Fairness: Schmidt & Sommerville 2011 PLoS ONE 15-month-olds expect equal sharing; Sloane, Baillargeon & Premack 2012 Psychological Science 19-month-olds.
  • Ingroup bias: Mahajan & Wynn 2012 (graham crackers vs green beans).
  • Paul Bloom "Just Babies" framing.

NATURE-NURTURE / TEMPERAMENT

  • Jerome Kagan: behavioral inhibition - 20% of 4-month-olds highly reactive; Kagan & Snidman; longitudinal outcomes (reactive infants more likely anxious adults).
  • Thomas & Chess New York Longitudinal Study (easy 40%, difficult 10%, slow-to-warm 15%).
  • Twin studies of infant temperament heritability (~20-60%).
  • Epigenetics / Meaney rat licking-and-grooming (Weaver 2004 Nature Neuroscience) - one line, flag extrapolation to humans.
  • Dunedin Study: Caspi/Moffitt - childhood self-control at 3-11 predicts adult health, wealth, crime (Moffitt 2011 PNAS, 1,000 children).
  • Culture and parenting: Lancy; Meehan; Hadza/Aka fathers (Aka fathers hold infants 22% of time, Hewlett); co-sleeping worldwide (~most societies); Gusii mothers rarely talk to infants (LeVine).
  • Birth order / parenting effects - flag.
  • Gender: parents' differential treatment; Mondschein, Adolph & Tamis-LeMonda 2000 "Gender bias in mothers' expectations about infant crawling" - mothers underestimated daughters and overestimated sons' crawling ability; actual performance did not differ.
  • Twin/ sibling: identical twins' babble synchrony - skip unless found.

DEBUNKED / OVERSTATED

  • Wonder Weeks (van de Rijt & Plooij; 10 "leaps"): the PhD student Carolina de Weerth failed to replicate the regression periods (de Weerth & van Geert 1998 Infant Behavior and Development; Plooij's conflict with Groningen, Plooij lost his job in 1998) - give specifics and flag.
  • Mozart effect (Rauscher 1993 Nature in college students, not babies; Pietschnig 2010 meta-analysis null; Georgia governor Zell Miller's CDs 1998).
  • Baby Einstein / video deficit (Zimmerman 2007; Disney refunds 2009) - brief if the other researcher covers; include the refund detail.
  • 30-million-word gap (Hart & Risley 1995; Sperry 2019 critique) - brief cross-reference.
  • Neonatal imitation failed replication (Oostenbroek 2016) - brief.
  • Vaccines-autism (Wakefield 1998 retracted) - one line.
  • "Babies don't feel pain" (surgery without anesthesia until 1980s; Anand 1987) - one line with number.
  • Sleep training harms? (Gradisar 2016 Pediatrics: no harm at 12 months; Price 2012 Pediatrics 5-year follow-up no harm) - flag solid/contested.
  • Teething causes fever (Massignan 2016) - brief if not covered.
  • Walkers/jumpers delay walking (Siegel & Burton 1999; Canada banned baby walkers 2004).
  • "Blank slate" - Pinker; babies born with core knowledge.
  • "Babies see upside down"? flag as myth with explanation.
  • Full-moon birth rates myth; "ten fingers ten toes" skip.
  • Left-brain/right-brain learning styles - skip.
  • Breast milk IQ - cross-ref only.
  • "Sleep regression" discrete stages - cross-ref: little peer-reviewed support.
  • Tummy-time evidence (Hewitt 2020 Pediatrics systematic review).
  • Pacifier/SIDS (De-Kun Li 2006 BMJ: ~90% reduction) and SIDS Back to Sleep (~50% drop since 1994).

Return a dense, well-organized Markdown list grouped under SOCIAL/BONDING / MORALITY / NATURE-NURTURE / DEBUNKED, ~150-200 lines, each fact with number + citation + URL + reliability flag. Note explicitly which Netflix "Babies" (2020) scientists you confirmed via press releases, interviews, or university news (quote the episode name if found).

Links: [{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3943240"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3247300"},{"title":"sciencedaily.com","url":"https://sciencedaily.com/releases/2010/08/100820101207.htm"},{"title":"Oxytocin, cortisol, and triadic family interactions","url":"https://cris.biu.ac.il/en/publications/oxytocin-cortisol-and-triadic-family-interactions-3/"},{"title":"The Natural Child Project The Natural Child Project","url":"https://www.naturalchild.org/research/oxytocin_dads.html"},{"title":"Bonding hormone seen in new dads, too","url":"https://www.naturalchild.org/articles/research/oxytocin_dads.html"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6838998/table/tbl2"},{"title":"oxytocin its a mom and pop thing","url":"https://scienceblog.com/oxytocin-its-a-mom-and-pop-thing/"},{"title":"testosterone oxytocin and the development of human parental care 3","url":"https://cris.biu.ac.il/en/publications/testosterone-oxytocin-and-the-development-of-human-parental-care-3/"},{"title":"scienceblog.com","url":"https://scienceblog.com/?p=37694"}]

The 2010 Biological Psychiatry paper, "Oxytocin and the Development of Parenting in Humans" by Gordon, Zagoory-Sharon, Leckman, and Feldman, matches your query. Its design was 160 cohabiting parents, meaning 80 couples, observed at home in the early postpartum weeks and again at 6 months. Plasma oxytocin was measured at each visit, and parent-infant interactions were coded for parenting behavior (Using a prospective longitudinal design, 160 cohabitating mothers and fathers and their firstborn infant were visited at home during the first postpartum weeks and again at 6 months postpartum.).

Key findings:
- Oxytocin levels rose over the study period, and mothers and fathers did not differ at either time point (Overall, parental OT increased across the study period and there were no differences between maternal and paternal OT at each time point.).
- Each parent's levels stayed fairly consistent over the six months, and spouses' levels were linked to each other (Oxytocin showed high intraindividual stability across the first 6 months of parenting and the OT levels of husband and wife were interrelated at both assessments.).
- Behavior differed by sex: maternal oxytocin tracked affectionate behaviors, while paternal oxytocin tracked stimulatory behaviors (Maternal OT was related to the amount of affectionate parenting behaviors... whereas paternal OT correlated with the degree of stimulatory parenting behaviors).

Press coverage describes the timing as 6 weeks and 6 months (They evaluated 160 first-time parents (80 couples) twice after the birth of their first child, at 6 weeks and 6 months). The paper itself says only "first postpartum weeks," so the press version is more specific than the abstract.

Don't confuse this with related papers by the same group. A 2010 Hormones and Behavior study on prolactin and oxytocin in fathers used a separate sample of 43 fathers (Forty-three fathers and their firstborn infant were seen twice: in the second and sixth postpartum months.).

Links: [{"title":"carta.anthropogeny.org","url":"https://carta.anthropogeny.org/node/308861"},{"title":"Father's brain is sensitive to childcare experiences","url":"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24912146/"},{"title":"fathers brain is sensitive to childcare experiences","url":"https://cris.biu.ac.il/en/publications/fathers-brain-is-sensitive-to-childcare-experiences/"},{"title":"anthropogeny.org","url":"https://anthropogeny.org/node/308861"},{"title":"scalda.mijn.bsl.nl","url":"https://scalda.mijn.bsl.nl/the-neural-basis-of-human-fatherhood-a-unique-biocultural-perspe/20104610"},{"title":"erasmusmc.mijn.bsl.nl","url":"https://erasmusmc.mijn.bsl.nl/the-neural-basis-of-human-fatherhood-a-unique-biocultural-perspe/20104610"},{"title":"z zapiskow mlodej lekarki","url":"https://ejournals.eu/en/journal/zdrowie-publiczne-i-zarzadzanie/article/z-zapiskow-mlodej-lekarki"},{"title":"frontiersin.org","url":"https://frontiersin.org/journals/behavioral-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnbeh.2022.820884/text"},{"title":"active parenting makes men more sensitive to child s needs","url":"https://pacesconnection.com/blog/active-parenting-makes-men-more-sensitive-to-child-s-needs"}]

The search confirms the study and its basic design, with one discrepancy in the group sizes.

- **Citation and authors:** The paper is Abraham, Hendler, Shapira-Lichter, Kanat-Maymon, Zagoory-Sharon and Feldman, published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences in 2014 (vol. 111, no. 27, pp. 9792–7). doi: 10.1073/pnas.1402569111
- **Sample:** The study compared three groups of parents (n = 89) raising their firstborn infant: primary-caregiving mothers, secondary-caregiving fathers, and primary-caregiving gay fathers raising infants without maternal involvement. The researchers used fMRI, oxytocin measures, and parenting behavior.
- **Group breakdown (discrepancy):** A PACEsConnection summary gives a different split, saying the 89 were 20 primary-caregiver heterosexual mothers and 21 secondary-caregiver heterosexual fathers along with 48 homosexual fathers. That press summary is secondary, so the paper's own group counts should be checked before relying on them.
- **Amygdala finding:** Primary-caregiving fathers exhibited high amygdala activation similar to PC-Mothers, alongside high activation of superior temporal sulcus (STS) comparable to SC-Fathers.
- **Childcare link:** Among all fathers, time spent in direct childcare was linked with the degree of amygdala-STS connectivity.
- **Other mother–father differences:** Mothers showed stronger activation in emotion-processing regions, while secondary-caregiving fathers showed stronger cortical activation.

The search didn't return the 1986 Tronick and Gianino paper itself, so I couldn't confirm its exact wording or the 30% figure from the original source. Related sources do report similar numbers.

- A 2011 Tronick co-authored study describes healthy mother-infant dyads as moving between matched and mismatched states, with mismatches quickly repaired. In healthy mother-infant dyads, interactions are characterized by a pattern of matching and mismatching interactive states with quick reparation of mismatches into matches.
- A twin-infant study that reviews earlier work says that more than 70% of the time of mother-infant interaction is spent in mismatching states, and the occurrence of coordination varies from not at all to 30%-40% of the interaction time. This is the closest match to your 30% figure, but it cites earlier work (refs 3 and 4) rather than the 1986 paper directly, and the search didn't identify those references.
- A popular book summary puts it differently, claiming approximately 70% of all parent-infant interactions are actually out of sync. That is a secondary source, so I'd treat it cautiously.

To verify the 1986 figure, look up the Tronick & Gianino paper directly, probably in *Infant Mental Health Journal* (1986), and check the matched-state percentage in its results.

The search confirms the core figures but didn't turn up the 1958 address text itself.

- **Date and venue:** Harlow presented "The nature of love" at the American Psychological Association's annual convention on August 31, 1958. Harlow first reported the results of these experiments in "The nature of love," the title of his address to the sixty-sixth Annual Convention of the American Psychological Association, Washington, DC, August 31, 1958.
- **Time with cloth vs. wire mothers:** One secondary source says the monkeys spent significantly more time with the cloth mother (approximately 17-18 hours per day) than with the wire mother (less than 1 hour per day), regardless of which mother provided food. A Comer textbook video page gives the same range: this baby spends from 17 to 18 hours a day on the cloth mother and less than one hour a day on the wire mother.
- **Source caveat:** The 17–18 hour figure is attributed in these sources to Harlow & Zimmerman (1959), "Affectional responses in the infant monkey," *Science*, not to the 1958 address. Harlow, H. F., & Zimmerman, R. R. (1959). Affectional responses in the infant monkey. Science, 130(3373), 421-432. I couldn't verify the figure against the primary papers, so if you need it for citation, check the original 1959 paper.
- **Wire mother's feeding:** Harlow's writing describes the wire-only mother as biologically adequate but psychologically inept. Per the search results, in the 1958 account the infants still preferred the cloth mother even though the wire mother dispensed milk.

The search turned up consistent core facts about the 1988 paper, but the exact pooled percentages vary across secondary sources, and I couldn't reach the original article's tables.

- **Design and sample:** The study covered 32 samples from 8 countries, with the USA contributing the most (18 samples). The total was N = 1,990, and the Leiden repository copy of the paper describes it as a meta-analysis of almost 2,000 Strange Situation classifications.
- **Pooled percentages:** One revision-style source gives an overall split of Secure 65% - Avoidant 21% - Resistant 14%. I could not confirm these figures against the paper itself, so treat them as secondary.
- **Country patterns:** The lowest percentage of secure attachments was shown in China, and the highest in Great Britain. Secondary sources also say Japan & Israel revealed a higher incidence of insecure-resistant than insecure-avoidant children. One source puts China's secure rate at 50%.
- **Within- vs. between-country variation:** Per one summary, Intra-cultural variation was nearly 15 times greater than the cross-cultural variations.
- **Conflicts to flag:** Some study-aid flashcards list country-level figures (e.g., UK secure 75%, USA 65%) that I could not verify. One flashcard also says only one baby was avoidant, which looks inconsistent with the other sources and may refer to a single country. I'd rely on the original paper's tables for exact numbers.

The search confirmed the core details of the 2007 *Science* paper, with one discrepancy on sample size.

- **Citation:** Nelson, Zeanah, Fox, Marshall, Smyke, and Guthrie, *Science* 318(5858), pp. 1937–1940, published December 2007 (Science. 2007 Dec 21;318(5858):1937-40. doi: 10.1126/science.1143921.).
- **Design:** It was a randomized controlled trial. Children in institutions were assigned either to stay in institutional care or to move to foster care, and their cognitive development was followed through 54 months (abandoned children reared in institutions were compared to abandoned children placed in institutions but then moved to foster care.).
- **Sample:** Most sources give 136 children. One source, Creating a Family, reports 187 children less than 31 months of age residing in six orphanages, which conflicts with the 136 figure. The 136 number is the one used in the study's own description and in the book by the same authors (136 abandoned infants and toddlers in the study, with half randomly assigned to foster care). The 187 figure may be a different count, such as the wider pool of institutionalized children, but I could not verify that from these results.
- **Findings:** Children who stayed in institutions scored well below both never-institutionalized children and children moved to foster care (The cognitive outcome of children who remained in the institution was markedly below that of never-institutionalized children and children taken out of the institution and placed into foster care.). Gains from foster care were largest for the youngest children placed (The improved cognitive outcomes we observed at 42 and 54 months were most marked for the youngest children placed in foster care.). The authors read this as evidence of a possible sensitive period in cognitive development (suggest a possible sensitive period in cognitive development).
- **Follow-up:** An 8-year follow-up was published in 2011 in *Journal of Child Psychology and Psychiatry* (Fox et al.). It found that cognitive gains from foster care were still present at age 8, which extends the 2007 results. The search returned only the abstract and introduction, so I cannot confirm the exact 8-year effect sizes.

The search turned up the full paper and several summaries. Charpak et al. (Pediatrics, 2017;139(1):e20162063) followed up a 1993–1996 Colombian randomized trial of kangaroo mother care (KMC) about 20 years later.

- **Main finding:** KMC had significant, long-lasting social and behavioral protective effects 20 years after the intervention.
- **Cognitive and home environment:** The 1-year effects on IQ and home environment were still present 20 years later in the most fragile individuals.
- **Behavior and school:** Parents in the KMC group were described as more protective and nurturing, which was linked to lower school absenteeism and less hyperactivity, aggressiveness, externalizing, and socio-deviant conduct in young adults (reduced school absenteeism and reduced hyperactivity, aggressiveness, externalization, and socio-deviant conduct of young adults).
- **Neuroimaging:** Neuroimaging showed larger volume of the left caudate nucleus in the KMC group.
- **Sample:** According to a clinical summary, from 2012 to 2014, a total of 494 (69%) of the 716 participants of the original randomized controlled trial (RCT) from 1993 to 1996 known to be alive were identified, and 441 were re-enrolled. The reported results come from the subgroup weighing 1800 g or less at birth, with results for the 264 participants weighing ≤1800 g at birth analyzed.
- **Conclusion:** The authors argue that coverage with this efficient and scientifically based health care intervention should be extended to the 18 million infants born each year who are candidates for the method.

Sources for the full text include the Pediatrics PDF mirrored at a Fiocruz portal and a ResearchGate copy. The 264-infant subgroup detail comes from a secondary summary, so check the full paper before citing those numbers.

The NEJM trial (WHO Immediate KMC Study Group, published May 27, 2021) is confirmed by several sources, though none of the results show the exact figures in your query, including the 3,211 infants.

- **Design:** The trial was a randomized controlled trial at five hospitals in Ghana, India, Malawi, Nigeria, and Tanzania. Infants weighing 1.0 to 1.799 kg were included. (Sources: 2-7, 8-1)
- **Intervention:** Immediate KMC started soon after birth, with infants averaging about 17 hours a day in skin-to-skin care, compared with about 1.5 hours a day in the control arm, where KMC began only after stabilization. (Sources: 6-2, 6-3)
- **Outcome:** Infants in the immediate group had lower 28-day mortality. Their rates of hypothermia and suspected sepsis were also lower, and the trial ended early. (Sources: 4-2, 4-4, 4-10)
- **Magnitude:** One source reports a 25% reduction in mortality, which is the figure your query cites. (Source: 5-4) A WHO-linked summary estimated that immediate KMC could save up to 150,000 more lives a year. (Source: 10-1)
- **Citation:** Published in NEJM 2021;384(21):2028–2038, doi 10.1056/NEJMoa2026486. (Source: 2-4, 2-5)

I could not confirm the 3,211 enrollment figure from these results. Check the full NEJM article for the exact sample size and the precise mortality numbers.

The search confirms the study and the figures you asked about, though the 74% and "none" numbers come from a secondary review rather than the 1985 paper itself.

- **Study:** Sorce, Emde, Campos and Klinnert (1985) is the paper, published in Developmental Psychology vol. 21, no. 1, pp. 195–200, under the title "Maternal emotional signaling: Its effect on the visual cliff behavior of 1-year-olds." Main Author: Sorce, James F, and the abstract reports that one-year-olds look to maternal facial expressions to guide their behavior (Results indicated that one-year-old infants seek out and use maternal facial expressions to monitor behavior.).
- **Design:** Twelve-month-old crawlers faced an apparent 30-cm drop-off covered by safety glass, and their mothers stood across the apparatus posing "happy" or "fearful" faces (Twelve-month-old crawling infants used mothers' facial expressions to determine whether to cross a 30-cm apparent drop-off. The risk of the drop-off was considered "ambiguous" because in pilot work, infants paused at the edge and looked to their mothers. The drop-off was apparent, not real, because safety glass over the surface protected infants from falling. During test trials, mothers stood at the far side of the apparatus and posed "happy" or "fearful" facial expressions.).
- **Result:** A Frontiers review citing the study reports that most infants (74%) crossed the visual cliff when mothers displayed a happy face but none crossed when mothers displayed fear. The same figures appear in a second copy of the review (Most infants (74%) crossed the visual cliff when mothers displayed a happy face but none crossed when mothers displayed fear.). I did not find the original paper's text to verify these numbers directly.
- **Critique:** The same review argues the 30-cm drop was probably not truly ambiguous for most infants, citing work showing that 30 cm is risky for most infants, not ambiguous (Kretch and Adolph, 2013).

The 2002 PNAS paper is "Eye contact detection in humans from birth" by Farroni, Csibra, Simion, and Johnson (PNAS 99(14): 9602–9605). The search turned up the abstract and some follow-up coverage.

**Design and findings:**
- The study ran two experiments. The first tested whether 2- to 5-day-old newborns could tell direct gaze from averted gaze. The second measured brain electrical activity in 4-month-olds viewing faces with direct versus averted gaze. (Two experiments were carried out to demonstrate special sensitivity to direct eye contact from birth. The first experiment tested the ability of 2- to 5-day-old newborns to discriminate between direct and averted gaze.)
- The authors concluded that newborns prefer faces that make mutual gaze, and that healthy 4-month-olds show stronger neural processing of direct gaze. (From birth, human infants prefer to look at faces that engage them in mutual gaze and that, from an early age, healthy babies show enhanced neural processing of direct gaze.)
- They argued this early sensitivity may underpin later social development. (The exceptionally early sensitivity to mutual gaze demonstrated in these studies is arguably the major foundation for the later development of social skills.)

**Context from other sources:**
- A 2018 review notes the same paper as evidence that newborns look longer at direct-gaze faces than at averted-gaze faces, and cites a 2006 follow-up on factors affecting this preference. (Even at birth when infants have poor visual acuity and ocular control, they have a preference for open over closed eyes (Batki et al., 2000) and direct over averted gaze (Farroni et al., 2002, 2006).)
- Press coverage at the time quoted Farroni on the study being the first to examine newborns' preference for direct-looking eyes. (This study, she says, is the first to examine newborns' preference for eyes that look right at them.) That is a claim from the lead author, not an independent verification.

The abstracts I found are consistent with each other. I did not access the full text, so details like sample sizes and methods are unverified here.

The search didn't surface the 1982 Martin & Clark paper itself. What I found comes from secondary sources, mainly a study of newborns of depressed mothers that cites it.

- **Original finding:** A later paper reports that several investigators, including Martin and Clark (1982), observed newborns crying in response to another newborn's cry, which they read as a possible empathic response. Newborns' crying in response to the cry of another newborn has been reported by several investigators (Martin & Clark, 1982; Sagi & Hoffman, 1976; Simner, 1971).
- **Own cry vs. other's cry:** According to that same source, the 1982 study found that infants cried to another newborn's cry but did not cry to their own recording, which the authors took as evidence that neonates can tell their own cry apart from others'. (Source: pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2031923.)
- **Later replication:** Dondi, Simion, and Caltran (1999) tested this directly. Their abstract reports that newborns showed the facial expression of distress more frequently and for a longer duration when hearing another infant's cry, and that the rate of sucking decreased significantly between the pretest phase and the 1st min of presentation of another infant's cry. A second experiment with sleeping newborns produced responses that were delayed and less intense.

To confirm the exact 1982 methods and results, you'd need the original Developmental Psychology article, which this search did not return.

- **Citation and authors:** The paper is Gettler, McDade, Feranil, and Kuzawa, published in PNAS in 2011, with the title "Longitudinal evidence that fatherhood decreases testosterone in human males" (volume 108, issue 39, pages 16194–16199). In species in which males care for young, testosterone (T) is often high during mating periods but then declines to allow for caregiving of resulting offspring.
- **Sample and design:** The team used a large representative study in the Philippines (n = 624). A press summary says the men were aged 21.5 to 26 years old and were followed for 4.5 years.

A later Cebu study adds that men who became new fathers but were not residing with their children also showed significantly greater declines in evening testosterone, relative to the comparison group.
- **Caveat on the 26%/34% figures:** I could not find these percentages in the results. Check the full paper (doi 10.1073/pnas.1105403108) before citing them.

The search turned up mostly press coverage of the study, not the paper itself, so the details below come from secondary reports.

- **Sample:** Researchers compared MRI scans of 25 first-time mothers before and after their pregnancy, of 19 male partners, and of a control group formed by 20 women who were not and had never been pregnant and 17 male partners.
- **Main finding:** Mothers showed significant reductions in grey matter in regions associated with social cognition. The affected areas were the medial frontal and posterior cortex line, as well as in specific sections of, mainly, prefrontal and temporal cortex.
- **Duration:** One press release says the changes were long-lasting – at least for two years post-partum. A different report describes the rescans as taking place about 15 months later, so the timing is inconsistent across sources and I'd check the paper itself.
- **Controls:** Fathers and non-pregnant women did not show comparable changes, which the researchers took to mean pregnancy itself drives the effect. Neither first-time fathers nor men and women who never had children experienced brain changes during the study.
- **Interpretation:** The authors suggested the change may be adaptive rather than a loss. Hoekzema said "Gray matter volume loss can also represent a beneficial process of maturation or specialization." No cognitive deficits were observed, per Researchers did not observe any changes in memory or other cognitive functions during the pregnancies.
- **Classification claim:** One report says the pattern was consistent enough that it correctly classified, with full accuracy, which women in the study had been pregnant and which hadn't. This comes from a secondary article, so treat it as unverified until checked against the paper.

I did not find the Nature Neuroscience paper itself or its DOI in these results.

The search confirmed the paper's basic details but did not return the sample numbers you asked about (24 infants, 22 helpers), so I can't verify those figures from these results.

- **Publication:** The paper is Warneken and Tomasello's "Altruistic helping in human infants and young chimpanzees," in *Science* vol. 311, no. 5765 (March 2006), pp. 1301–1303. The DOI is 10.1126/science.1121448.
- **Core finding:** The authors report that 18-month-olds help others reach goals across several situations, and that three young chimpanzees showed similar but less consistent helping. They found human children as young as 18 months of age quite readily help others to achieve their goals in a variety of situations. In addition, they demonstrated similar though less robust skills and motivations in three young chimpanzees.
- **Task design:** In a Max Planck press release, Warneken described a scenario in which he dropped a clothes peg out of reach and then said he wanted it. He did not directly ask the child for help, and did not thank or reward the child if the peg was retrieved. That release also reports that virtually all children helped at least once in these situations, and in 84% of cases they helped during the first 10 seconds, before Warneken even made eye contact.
- **Control conditions:** Secondary coverage notes that infants and chimps rarely helped in control conditions, such as when the researcher deliberately threw something on the floor.

For the exact sample size and the 22-of-24 count, check the full text or supplementary material at the DOI above.

The search confirmed the 1978 paper and the basic procedure, but the sources disagree on the journal.

**Citation:** Most sources give the authors as Tronick, Als, Adamson, Wise, and Brazelton, with pages 1–13 of volume 17 (1978). One source, a Psychology Today piece, lists the venue as *Pediatrics*. Another, a Springer reference list, lists the *Journal of the American Academy of Child Psychiatry*. The Springer listing is the one I'd trust, but I'd verify it against the original before citing it. Tronick, Edward, Heidelise Als, Lauren Adamson, Susan Wise and T. Berry Brazelton. "The Infant's Response to Entrapment between Contradictory Messages in Face-to-face Interaction." Pediatrics, September 1978, vol. 17(1), pp. 1-13. versus Tronick, E., Als, H., Adamson, L., Wise, S., and Brazelton, T. B. (1978) 'The infant's response to entrapment between contradictory messages in face-to-face interaction', Journal of the American Academy of Child Psychiatry 17, 1–13.

**Three-minute structure:** Later studies describe the standard procedure as a normal interaction episode (3 min); the "still-face" episode in which the mother became unresponsive and assumed a neutral expression (1 min); a reunion episode in which the mother resumed the interaction (3 min). Some studies use different timings. One Italian study with 3–6-month-olds used 2 min for each phase.

**Infant response:** Tronick's description of the infant after three minutes of non-responsiveness, as quoted in a Scientific American post, is that the infant "rapidly sobers and grows wary." He makes repeated attempts to get the interaction into its usual reciprocal pattern. The same post says that when those attempts fail, the infant withdraws [and] orients his face and body away from his mother with a withdrawn, hopeless facial expression."

**Context:** The 1978 paper's framing, that infants are caught between contradictory messages, is discussed in a UMass dissertation-style document, which argues that displays which convey contradictory messages violate the rules governing the exchange and that such violations produce negative affect and disturbance in the infant. I did not retrieve the full 1978 text, so I can't confirm its exact wording or methods.

The search confirmed the study's basic design and findings, but it did not return the per-group counts you asked about (14 of 16 and 12 of 12).

- **What the results confirm:** A 2015 Frontiers review by Hamlin describes the 2007 paper as the first evidence that infants at 6 and 10 months evaluate others by their helpful and unhelpful acts toward third parties. A Climber puppet tried but failed to climb a steep hill, and was alternately bumped up the hill by the Helper and bumped down the hill by the Hinderer. After habituation, both 10- and 6-month-olds selectively reached for the Helper over the Hinderer.
- **Critique:** Scarf et al. (2012) argued that the choices reflected a low-level perceptual preference for the character associated with the Climber's bouncing, not social evaluation (cited in Hamlin's 2015 paper, which describes infants' choices as reflecting low-level perceptual preferences whereby infants are drawn to any character associated with the Climber bouncing).
- **Replication:** A 2020 replication with 14- to 16-month-olds found that only 16 out of 32 infants (50%) reached for the helper, so the original findings were not replicated.

I could not verify the 14/16 and 12/12 figures from these results. From memory, the 2007 paper reported 14 of 16 6-month-olds and 12 of 12 10-month-olds choosing the helper, but please check the Nature paper (doi:10.1038/nature06288) before relying on those numbers.

The ManyBabies 4 study was published in *Developmental Science*, not *Nature*. The original 2007 helper-hinderer study by Hamlin and colleagues appeared in *Nature*.

- **Main result:** The study found no evidence of a helper preference. Links: [{"title":"Probing the Strength of Infants' Preference for Helpers over Hinderers: Two Replication Attempts of Hamlin and Wynn (2011)","url":"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4643979/"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4643979"},{"title":"energy.ceu.edu","url":"https://energy.ceu.edu/sites/default/files/publications/salvadorietal2015.pdf"},{"title":"PLoS ONE (Jan 2015)","url":"https://doaj.org/article/4f86d3884aae401baaabb5857bc638c7"},{"title":"energy.ceu.edu","url":"https://energy.ceu.edu/node/44221.html"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3414446"},{"title":"www.frontiersin.org","url":"https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2014.01563/pdf"},{"title":"Campus Access Only","url":"https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/uop_etds/294"},{"title":"www.frontiersin.org","url":"https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2014.01563/text"}]

I ran one search. It did not turn up the 2011 PNAS paper, the 3-month-old Developmental Science study, or the 8-month-old puppet result you named, so I can't confirm those details.

What the results do cover is a related Hamlin and Wynn (2011) helper/hinderer study. In that paradigm, infants were habituated to a live puppet show in which a protagonist tried to open a box to retrieve a toy placed inside. The protagonist was either helped by a second puppet (the "Helper"), or hindered by a third puppet (the "Hinderer"). The original report is described as finding that 75% of 9-month-olds selected the Helper. That age differs from the 8-month-olds in your query, so the source you're citing may be a different paper or the details may be off.

Replication work also raises doubts about the effect. Salvadori et al. (2015) ran two replications and found that 9-month-olds' preference for helpers over hinderers did not differ significantly from chance (62.5% and 50%, respectively, in Studies 1 and 2). Separately, a 2011 study by other authors noted that no independent research teams have published a replication of these methods and results up to that point, and it reported its own replication attempt.

The earlier foundational work is Hamlin, Wynn & Bloom (2007), which tested 6- and 10-month-olds in a "hill paradigm." After being habituated to these events, both 10- and 6-month-olds selectively reached for the Helper over the Hinderer.

To verify the PNAS 2011 and Developmental Science 3-month-old claims, search the paper titles directly on PNAS or Wiley, or check Karen Wynn's or Kiley Hamlin's publication lists.

The search turned up the paper's bibliographic details and abstract, but not the specific numerical results (sample sizes, test statistics, p-values) you asked for.

- **Citation details:** The article was published in PLoS ONE on October 7, 2011, by Marco F. H. Schmidt and Jessica A. Sommerville, with DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0023223 and volume 6, issue 10 (e23223). The authors are affiliated with the Max Planck Institute for Evolutionary Anthropology in Leipzig and the University of Washington in Seattle.
- **Main findings (abstract):** The study tested 15-month-old infants' sensitivity to fairness, and their altruistic behavior, assessed via infants' reactions to a third-party resource distribution task, and via a sharing task. The authors reported that 15-month-old infants are sensitive to fairness and can engage in altruistic sharing. They also found that infants' degree of sensitivity to fairness as a third-party observer was related to whether they shared toys altruistically or selfishly.
- **Method, from press coverage:** One news report says that the study involved showing two short videos to 15-month-old babies. In those videos, a bowl of crackers was distributed between two people - first with an equal allocation of crackers, and then with one person getting more crackers than the other. Looking time was the measure, and babies spent more time looking at the allocation of food if one person got more than the other.

For the exact numbers, the full text is the place to look. The PMC version (PMC3188955) and the Konstanz repository PDF should have the sample sizes, looking-time statistics, and sharing results.

Links: [{"title":"Campus Access Only","url":"https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/uop_etds/4171"},{"title":"Date of Award","url":"https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/uop_etds/4242"},{"title":"scholarlycommons.pacific.edu","url":"https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/purcc/2017/events/67"},{"title":"scholarlycommons.pacific.edu","url":"https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/cop-facpres/1154"},{"title":"Science & Technology","url":"https://news.yale.edu/2013/03/12/infants-prefer-individuals-who-punish-those-not-themselves-yale-researchers-find"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4374623"},{"title":"Academic Journal","url":"https://discovery-imamu.kwaretech.com/EdsRecord/eric,EJ971178"},{"title":"Preference for Similar Others: A Replication and Extension","url":"https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/cop-facpres/1151"},{"title":"sciencedaily.com","url":"https://sciencedaily.com/releases/2013/03/130312092920.htm"},{"title":"Your new experience awaits. Try the new design now and help us make it even better","url":"https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2019.02239/full"}]

The search didn't return the original Cognition paper, so these findings come from secondary sources: replications, abstracts, and press coverage.

- **Design:** Mahajan and Wynn (2012) asked infants seated in their parents' lap (n = 32), to choose between two foods, watch a puppet show during which two puppets verbally stated a liking or disliking of these foods, and then choose one of the puppets.
- **Result:** More infants (87.5%) chose the puppet that "liked" the same food the infants chose, which led the researchers to suggest this tendency is innate. A 2015 poster reports the figure as 84%, and says the effect depended on salience: infants who chose the food first (high salience) chose the similar puppet 84% of the time, compared to 44% when they chose a puppet and then a food (low salience).
- **Replication concerns:** One replication notes that methodological limitations, including the unintentional effects of parent bias and use of a single choice-trial, make this conclusion premature. A separate replication found that results may have been affected by factors other than infant preference such as parental bias or side bias.
- **Age:** None of the retrieved sources confirm 11-month-olds for this study. The 9- and 14-month-old groups appear in a different study, Hamlin and colleagues (2013), which used the graham cracker/green bean design and added helper and hinderer puppets. Almost all the infants in both the 9- and 14-month-old groups preferred the character who helped the similar puppet over the character who harmed the similar puppet. When the puppet that dropped the ball did not share the babies' taste in food, the infants preferred the mean puppet to the helper.
- **Citation:** The paper is listed as Cognition, Aug 2012, 124(2):227-233, with the title "Origins of 'Us' versus 'Them': Prelinguistic Infants Prefer Similar Others." Its DOI is 10.1016/j.cognition.2012.05.003.

The 11-month age in your query could not be verified from these results, so check the paper itself before relying on it.

The search confirms the book and its core findings, but it did not turn up the specific "4 months" or "20 percent" details you asked about.

- **The book:** Kagan and Snidman's *The Long Shadow of Temperament* (Harvard University Press, 2004) summarizes one of the best-known longitudinal studies in developmental psychology. Kagan and his colleagues returned to these children as adolescents after identifying extreme types in infancy and childhood.
- **Infant-to-adulthood link:** One infant temperament predicted a cautious, fearful personality in early childhood and a dour mood in adolescence. The other predicted a bold childhood personality and an exuberant, sanguine mood in adolescence.
- **Biology:** The authors conclude that these two temperaments are the result of inherited biologies probably rooted in the differential excitability of particular brain structures.
- **Group size:** A Harvard Gazette profile says the high- and low-reactive groups each comprised about 25 percent of the children, whereas the majority fell in the middle. That conflicts with the 20 percent figure in your query, so the 20 percent number needs checking against the primary papers.
- **Adult anxiety:** The Gazette notes that others proved at risk for developing diagnosable anxiety disorders in adolescence and beyond, typically social anxiety disorder.
- **Not found:** None of the results gave the 4-month screening protocol or the exact cutoff for classifying infants as high-reactive. Those details would need the original 1980s–1990s papers or the book itself.

The 2000 study is indexed on PubMed, and its abstract matches your description.

- **Design:** Mothers of 11-month-old infants estimated their babies' crawling ability, crawling attempts, and motor decisions in a novel locomotor task of crawling down steep and shallow slopes.
- **Main finding:** The abstract reports that mothers of girls underestimated their performance and mothers of boys overestimated their performance.
- **Infant performance:** The mothers' gender bias had no basis in fact. When the infants were tested on the same slope task moments after the mothers gave their ratings, girls and boys showed identical levels of motor performance.
- **Sample size:** A German news article (Heidelberg24) reports 23 mother-child pairs were tested and that nine of twelve mothers underestimated their daughters' crawling performance. This is a secondary source, so I'd check the full paper for the exact N.
- **Later citation:** Ishak, Tamis-LeMonda, and Adolph cite the study for the point that mothers of 11-month-old crawlers expected their infants to attempt slopes that were steeper than they could safely navigate.

The search did not return the full text, so details such as the exact sample size, statistics, and effect sizes are unverified.

- The abstract states: "Another follow-up session showed that the GM reductions endured for at least 2 years post-pregnancy" (about 99 characters).

**Duration of changes**
- Gray matter reductions lasted at least 2 years after pregnancy, per the abstract quoted above. The introduction gives the same figure, "at least two years post-partum."

**Brain regions**
- The article reports "a symmetrical reduction in the volume of grey matter in the medial frontal and posterior cortex line" (about 100 characters).
- It also describes changes in "specific sections of, mainly, prefrontal and temporal cortex" in pregnant women.

**Classification accuracy**
- The abstract says the changes were "highly consistent, correctly classifying all women as having undergone pregnancy or not in-between sessions" (the quoted portion is about 89 characters). The article does not report a numeric accuracy percentage.

**Journal and DOI**
- Journal: *Nature Neuroscience*.
- DOI: 10.1038/nn.4458.
- Published online December 19, 2016.

The search did not confirm the specific Baltimore figures (n = 106; roughly 66% secure, 22% avoidant, 12% ambivalent). None of the results report those numbers, so they remain unverified here. The results do cover the book and classification scheme:

- **Study and book:** The book reports the methods and key results of Ainsworth's Baltimore Longitudinal Study, and it is listed as a Psychology Press/Routledge Classic Edition. (Routledge and Lavoisier listings, 4-4 and 3-4)
- **Classification:** Infants were sorted by their behavior in the Strange Situation, especially during reunion episodes. (Miami PDF, 5-4)
- **Avoidant (A):** Infants largely ignore the returning caregiver or mix greeting with avoidance, such as turning away or averting gaze. (Safer Society criteria, 6-1 and 6-2)
- **Ambivalent/resistant (C):** These infants struggle to explore away from the mother, are highly distressed by separation, and are hard to settle at reunion. A cited Child Development passage notes that about one in seven babies in normal samples shows this pattern. (Leiden PDF, 9-3 to 9-6)

For the exact sample size and percentages, check the original 1978 book or a secondary source that reports the Baltimore distribution directly.

The 1999 meta-analysis is confirmed. Its abstract reports that in normal middle class families about 15% of the infants develop disorganized attachment behavior, and that the rate may become twice or even three times higher in other social contexts and clinical groups, such as maltreatment. The paper appeared in Development and Psychopathology, volume 11, pages 225-249.

A later source reports the same contrast: 48 per cent of maltreated children were classified as insecure-disorganized, compared with 15 per cent of children from regular middle-class families. A 2019 meta-analysis found that the weighted mean rate ranged from 15% in community samples to 28% and 30% in at-risk samples.

Links: [{"title":"Epigenetic programming by maternal behavior","url":"https://www.research.ed.ac.uk/en/publications/epigenetic-programming-by-maternal-behavior/"},{"title":"Weaver2004Epigenetic programming by maternal behavior","url":"https://www.nig.ac.jp/jimu/soken/courses/devbiol/2021/Weaver2004Epigenetic_programming_by_maternal_behavior.pdf"},{"title":"nature.com","url":"https://www.nature.com/articles/nrn1483"},{"title":"www.nature.com","url":"https://www.nature.com/articles/nrn1832.pdf"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6725868"},{"title":"Parentage has effects outside the genome","url":"https://www.the-scientist.com/parentage-has-effects-outside-the-genome-47781"},{"title":"utexas.edu","url":"https://labs.la.utexas.edu/champagne/files/2018/01/champ5.pdf"},{"title":"maternal programming of glucocorticoid receptor expression and hp","url":"https://dalhousie-university2.demo.elsevierpure.com/zh/publications/maternal-programming-of-glucocorticoid-receptor-expression-and-hp"},{"title":"elsevierpure.com","url":"https://dalhousie-university2.demo.elsevierpure.com/en/publications/maternal-programming-of-glucocorticoid-receptor-expression-and-hp/fingerprints"}]

The 2004 paper is confirmed: Weaver, Cervoni, Champagne, D'Alessio, Sharma, Seckl, Dymov, Szyf and Meaney, "Epigenetic programming by maternal behavior," *Nature Neuroscience*, 27 June 2004 (doi:10.1038/nn1276).

- **Core finding:** Rat mothers' pup licking/grooming and arched-back nursing shaped the offspring's epigenome at a glucocorticoid receptor gene promoter in the hippocampus. Increased pup licking and grooming (LG) and arched-back nursing (ABN) by rat mothers altered the offspring epigenome at a glucocorticoid receptor (GR) gene promoter in the hippocampus.
- **Methylation differences:** Offspring of mothers that showed high levels of LG and ABN were found to have differences in DNA methylation, as compared to offspring of 'low-LG-ABN' mothers. The paper's abstract adds that these differences appeared in the first week of life, were reversed by cross-fostering, and persisted into adulthood. They were also linked to altered histone acetylation and NGFI-A binding at the GR promoter (index 1-3).
- **Behavioral link:** Adult offspring of high-LG-ABN mothers showed less fearfulness and more modest HPA stress responses than offspring of low-LG-ABN mothers (index 2-6).
- **Follow-up work:** A later study by Weaver and colleagues (2005, *J Neurosci*) infused methionine into adult offspring to test whether this methylation pattern could be reversed (index 5-3, 5-4).

I didn't retrieve the full text of the 2004 paper, so the details above come from the abstract and a PDF excerpt. The sources don't state the exact rat numbers or methods, so check the full paper for those.

Links: [{"title":"www.frontiersin.org","url":"https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/genetics/articles/10.3389/fgene.2012.00009/pdf"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1188235"},{"title":"0% found this document useful (0 votes)","url":"https://www.scribd.com/document/683350737/nihms2462"},{"title":"About the Authors:","url":"https://www.proquest.com/docview/1990012644"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5407782"},{"title":"Научная статья на тему 'Genetic and environmental influences on temperament in adolescence'","url":"https://cyberleninka.ru/article/n/genetic-and-environmental-influences-on-temperament-in-adolescence"},{"title":"genetic and environmental 8","url":"https://uva.theopenscholar.com/files/eric-turkheimer/files/genetic_and_environmental_8.pdf"},{"title":"genetic analyses of focal aspects of infant temperament","url":"https://pure.psu.edu/en/publications/genetic-analyses-of-focal-aspects-of-infant-temperament/"},{"title":"resolve.cambridge.org","url":"https://resolve.cambridge.org/core/journals/twin-research-and-human-genetics/article/parent-ratings-of-temperament-in-twins-explaining-the-too-low-dz-correlations/B02D300B0F9363DF1D7AE77948961623"}]

The search turned up useful context, but not the exact 20%–60% infant figures you asked about.

- **Saudino (2005):** Her review states that twin and adoption studies suggest infant and child temperament is genetically influenced, and argues that behavioral genetics offers more than simple heritability estimates, including nonshared environment and links to behavior problems. The source is a J Dev Behav Pediatr article with a PMC copy.
- **Goldsmith et al. (1997), as cited by a later paper:** A Frontiers review says Goldsmith and colleagues ran a meta-analysis of weighted intraclass correlations from parent temperament questionnaires. The primary 1997 meta-analysis itself did not appear in results.
- **Goldsmith, Lemery, Buss & Campos (twin study of infant temperament):** The Penn State record describes a sample of 604 infant twins aged 3 to 16 months. It found additive genetic effects for some IBQ scales (such as Distress to Novelty and Activity Level) and shared environmental effects for others (such as Soothability).
- **Negative emotionality in toddlers:** A twin-study paper reports heritability of 62% at 14 months and 29% at 20 months for observed negative emotionality (Rhee et al.), and 37% rising to 47% then falling to 11% for parent-reported negative emotionality between 14 and 36 months (Saudino & Cherney). Estimates shift considerably with age and rater type.
- **Measurement caveat:** A Cambridge Twin Research paper notes that parent ratings often produce DZ correlations that are too low. This can inflate heritability estimates through contrast effects. Its model fitting favored contrast effects, and it also says objective measures consistently suggest moderate heritability.
- **Range:** A review of adolescent temperament reports about 30–60% of variance explained by genetic factors. That range comes from adolescents, not infants, so it does not confirm the 20%–60% figure for infants.

Overall, the results support moderate heritability for infant temperament, with estimates that vary by age, rater, and measurement method. I could not verify the specific 20%–60% infant range from the sources retrieved. For that, the Goldsmith 1997 meta-analysis and Saudino 2005 full text would need to be checked directly.

Here's what the one search turned up on the Pietschnig, Voracek, and Formann meta-analysis. I did not search the Rauscher 1993 or Zell Miller claims, so those remain unverified.

**Study count and sample size differ across sources.** The ScienceDaily write-up says the team retrieved about 40 independent studies, published ones as well as a number of unpublished academic theses, totalling more than 3000 participants. A French-language abstract database record and a secondary exam-prep page both say 36 studies met inclusion criteria, with a total of 3,000 participants. Your query's figure of 39 studies doesn't match either source, so the journal article itself is the place to confirm the exact number.

**Citation details.** The paper is listed as "Mozart effect–Shmozart effect: A meta-analysis." Intelligence, 38, 314-323.

**Main findings.** The authors reported that formal tests yielded evidence for confounding publication bias, requiring downward correction of effects. They also found that the noticeably higher overall effect in studies performed by Rauscher and colleagues than in studies performed by other researchers points to lab affiliation as a moderator. Their overall conclusion was that there is little evidence left for a specific, performance-enhancing Mozart effect.

**Rauscher 1993.** The ScienceDaily piece says a specific "Mozart effect," as suggested by Rauscher's 1993 publication in Nature, could not be confirmed. I did not verify the 36-student sample or the 8–9 IQ point gain.

**Zell Miller / Georgia CDs.** Not searched, so I have no sourced information on this point.

The Oostenbroek et al. (2016) paper is in *Current Biology* 26, pp. 1334–1338, and the search confirmed the core details you listed.

- **Design:** The researchers presented infants (n = 106) with nine social and two non-social models and scored their responses at 1, 3, 6, and 9 weeks of age. The nine social gestures were four facial (tongue protrusion, mouth opening, happy face, sad face), two hand (index finger protrusion, grasping), and three vocal (“mmm,” “eee,” tongue clicks). The two non-social models were a tube simulating tongue protrusion and a box simulating mouth opening (A female adult experimenter modeled nine social gestures... Additionally, two nonsocial models were included to test alternative interpretations).
- **Attrition:** Only data from 64 of the infants was available at all four time points.
- **Main finding:** The infants did not imitate any of the models. They produced the gestures about as often in response to control models as to matching ones (Longitudinal analyses indicated that the infants did not imitate any of the models).
- **Follow-up dispute:** A later post-hoc reanalysis of the same data by Meltzoff et al. (2017) argued that the infants actually did imitate one of the nine actions: tongue protrusion. Meltzoff's group also argued the design was too brief to show neonates the stimulus or let them respond (The test periods were too brief. The stimulus presentation for each type of gesture was too short to ensure that neonates saw the display.). The original authors rejected those points as post hoc (In their commentary, Oostenbroek et al. (2018) argue that our points are post hoc.).

- A later review describes it as a landmark that challenged the belief that babies do not feel pain, and it recommended giving local and general anesthesia during invasive procedures ([PMC7350116](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7350116)).

- A Harvard newsletter reports that infants circumcised without analgesia showed heightened aversion to needle procedures months later ([HMS](https://hms.harvard.edu/sites/default/files/publications%20archive/OnTheBrain/OnTheBrainWinter11.pdf)).

**Lancet 1987 (fentanyl trial)**
- The Lancet paper is "Randomised trial of fentanyl anaesthesia in preterm babies undergoing surgery: effects on the stress response," Lancet 1987;1(8524):62–66 ([PMC1721820](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1721820)). It concerns fentanyl, not halothane.
- A Lancet letter, "Pain, anaesthesia, and babies," appeared in 1987 as well ([Cambridge](https://resolve.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/essentials-of-anesthesia-for-infants-and-neonates/anestheticinduced-neurotoxicity/000C1027B2E2DE8496B8638D2A62646D)).

**Halothane study**
- The halothane paper is "Does halothane anaesthesia decrease the metabolic and endocrine stress responses of newborn infants undergoing operation?" Br Med J 1988;296(6623):668–672 ([PMC1678876](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1678876)). Its published conclusion is that potent anaesthesia with halothane should be given to neonates undergoing surgery unless contraindicated, because it reduces stress responses ([PMC2545294](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2545294)).

**Paralysis**
- I found nothing in these results about infants being paralyzed in any of these studies. That detail should be checked against the full texts before it is repeated.

Both studies are real and match your details, with one correction on the sample size. Gradisar's trial enrolled 43 infants, and the 326 figure comes from the Price group's cohort work.

**Gradisar et al. (Pediatrics, 2016):** The trial randomized 43 infants (6-16 months, 63% girls) to graduated extinction, bedtime fading, or a sleep education control. Both active methods shortened the time to fall asleep. Cortisol fell modestly in both groups relative to controls, and no significant differences were found in emotional and behavioral problems, and no significant differences in secure-insecure attachment styles between groups at 12 months. The authors concluded that the methods convey no adverse stress responses or long-term effects on parent-child attachment or child emotions and behavior. The sample is small, so these null findings have limited statistical power.

**Price et al. (Pediatrics, 2012):** This is a 5-year follow-up of a cluster-randomized trial. Three hundred twenty-six children (173 intervention) with parent-reported sleep problems at age 7 months were selected from a population sample of 692 infants recruited from well-child centers. The authors reported that behavioral sleep techniques did not cause long-lasting harms or benefits to child, child-parent, or maternal outcomes. A separate Sleep Medicine paper from the same group used a community-based cohort of 326 six-year-olds to examine whether infant sleep problems predict later outcomes. That is a different analysis, not the Pediatrics trial itself.

The search confirms the core figures from the Massignan et al. 2016 meta-analysis, and the fever finding is consistent across sources.

- **Prevalence:** Pooled data from 16 studies put the overall rate of eruption-related signs and symptoms in children aged 0 to 36 months at 70.5%, with a 95% CI of 54.1–84.6 (Meta-analysis revealed that the overall prevalence of signs and symptoms associated with primary tooth eruption in children between 0 and 36 months was 70.5% (95%CI; 54.1-84.6)).
- **Most common symptoms:** Gingival irritation (86.81%), irritability (68.19%), and drooling (55.72%) led the list (The most frequent signs and symptoms included gingival irritation (86.81 percent), irritability (68.19 percent), and drooling (55.72 percent).).
- **Fever:** The authors reported that eruption could raise body temperature, but that the rise was not characterized as fever. A commentary in ADC noted that only three studies recorded daily temperatures, with differences between eruption and non-eruption days ranging from zero to 0.7°C, and a highest rectal reading of 37.8°C (temperature differences between eruption and non-eruption days were between zero and 0.7°C, and the highest (rectal) temperature reported was 37.8°C). A 2018 review found the same pattern of a slight rise without fever, but noted heterogeneity in how fever was defined and measured (significant heterogeneity amongst studies in the definition of fever).
- **Discrepancy:** Some secondary write-ups say the meta-analysis covered ten studies, which conflicts with the 16 reported in the abstract-level sources. I'd rely on the 16-study figure.

I did not find the exact 38°C wording in the paper itself. The ADC excerpt cuts off right at its 38°C statement, so I could not confirm the authors' precise threshold language. The full paper at doi:10.1542/peds.2015-3501 would settle that.

The search confirmed the study details. Summary of findings from Hewitt, Kerr, Stanley, and Okely (Pediatrics, 2020;145(5):e20192168):

- **Scope:** The review pooled 16 articles covering 4,237 participants from eight countries. Investigators included peer-reviewed English-language articles that examined healthy infants who were aged 0 to 12 months.
- **Positive associations:** Reviewers found benefits for gross motor and total development, a lower BMI z-score, and prevention of brachycephaly. Tummy time also linked to better movement while prone, supine, crawling, and rolling. (Sources: contemporarypediatrics.com, index 3-6; braceworks.ca, index 6-2 and 6-5)
- **Indeterminate:** Social and cognitive domains, plagiocephaly, walking, standing, and sitting showed no clear conclusion. (index 3-7; 6-6)
- **No association:** Fine motor development and communication showed no link. (index 3-8; 6-7)
- **Limitations:** Most included studies were observational, relied on questionnaires to measure tummy time, and had selection and performance bias. The lead author called for more rigorous designs and objective measurement. (index 1-5, 1-6; 3-9)

One caveat: the 4,237 figure and the 16-article count are consistent across the secondary coverage I found, but I did not access the Pediatrics paper itself, so the exact per-outcome study counts should be checked against the full text.

The 2006 BMJ study reports the odds ratio you cited, and the SIDS decline figures vary by source and end year.

**Li et al. 2006 (BMJ):** The adjusted odds ratio for SIDS with a dummy used during the last sleep was 0.08 (95% CI 0.03 to 0.21). The study was a case-control design in eleven California counties, with 185 SIDS infants and 312 matched controls. Use was associated with a reduction in risk in every category of sociodemographic characteristics and risk factors examined. The "90% reduction" wording comes from press coverage, not the paper itself; one news report described the finding as a 90% reduction in risk. An odds ratio of 0.08 is roughly a 92% reduction in odds, so "90%" is a loose rounding.

**Back to Sleep and SIDS decline:** The sources disagree on the figure and the timeframe:
- NICHD's Safe to Sleep infographic says the U.S. SIDS rate dropped more than 50% between 1994 and 2016.
- The Nation's Health, citing federal statistics, says since the launch of the Back to Sleep campaign in 1994, sudden infant death syndrome, or SIDS, has declined by more than 50 percent in the United States.
- A Connecticut health department page says SIDS rates have dropped over 40 percent nationwide.
- A NICHD-sourced slide deck gives a larger figure, the overall United States SIDS rate declined by more than 60%, but it is tied to data through 2011 and a different period.

The CDC vaccine-safety page I found describes the decline as dramatic but gives no percentage, so I could not confirm a CDC-stated figure. The best-supported figure is "more than 50% from 1994 to roughly 2016," with the NICHD source being the most direct.

The 2010 paper is Hamlin, Wynn and Bloom, "Three-month-olds show a negativity bias in their social evaluations," *Developmental Science* 13(6), 923–929. The search confirms its core claim and gives a looking-time summary, though not the full methods.

- **Main finding:** Two experiments suggest that 3-month-olds judge others by how they treat third parties, and that negative social information gets privileged treatment. (Results from two experiments indicate that even 3-month-old infants evaluate others based on their social behavior towards third parties, and that negative social information is developmentally privileged.)
- **Looking-time measure:** A later critique describes this study as the first follow-up to the 2007 hill paradigm, in which the researchers tested 3-month-old infants using the hill paradigm and measured looking time, rather than object choice, to assess infants' preference. The critique adds that these infants displayed a significant preference for the helper when shown the helper and hinderer. The snippet cuts off there, so I can't confirm the exact looking-time figures or the negativity-bias contrast from this result.
- **Context:** The 2007 work used 6- and 10-month-olds. Its hill-paradigm results were challenged on perceptual grounds: one study found that when the Climber bounced both after being helped and after being hindered, infants chose randomly between the Helper and Hinderer. The original authors responded to that critique.

I didn't find the paper's full methods or effect sizes, so for the looking-time details, check the PMC full text (PMC2966030).

The search turned up the paper's basic design and findings, though not the full results.

- **Publication:** Sloane, Baillargeon, and Premack, "Do Infants Have a Sense of Fairness?", *Psychological Science* 23(2), 2012, pp. 196–204. The researchers focused on the norm of fairness and examined 19- to 21-month-old infants' expectations about individuals' actions in two contexts: the allocation of resources and the dispensation of rewards for effort.
- **Experiment 1 (19-month-olds):** Infants saw two giraffe puppets and an experimenter who gave out two toys, either one to each puppet or both to one. Infants were timed gazing at the scene until they lost interest, and longer looking times signaled that something was unexpected to the baby. A Sloane thesis summary reports that the 19-month-olds expected the experimenter to divide two items equally, as opposed to unequally, between two individuals. That same summary says infants held no particular expectation when the individuals were replaced with inert objects, which points to the expectation being specific to agents.
- **Experiment 2 (21-month-olds):** This experiment tested expectations about rewarding effort. The thesis abstract says 21-month-olds expected an experimenter to give a reward to, but the snippet cuts off before the rest, so I could not confirm the details.
- **Replication context:** A later paper describes the design this way: in one trial the experimenter made an equal distribution, giving one object to each puppet, whereas in the unequal scenario the experimenter gave both objects to one of the puppets. That same paper reports that the toddlers looked reliably longer at the test scene after witnessing the unequal distribution.

For the full Experiment 2 results and effect sizes, the original article is at the University of Miami PDF (psy.miami.edu) and the thesis is on the Illinois IDEALS repository.

Links: [{"title":"scholars.duke.edu","url":"https://scholars.duke.edu/publication/1458228"},{"title":"Angol nyelvu szakcikkminta 1","url":"https://ppkdocs.elte.hu/th/felveteli/letoltheto_dokumentumok_2022/pszicho_MA_mintacikkek/Angol_nyelvu_szakcikkminta_1"},{"title":"Psychology and Neuroscience Faculty Database","url":"https://fds.duke.edu/db/aas/pn/faculty/michael.tomasello/publications/351818"},{"title":"Evolutionary Anthropology Faculty Database","url":"https://fds.duke.edu/db/aas/BAA/faculty%20all/michael.tomasello/publications/351818"},{"title":"Linguistics Faculty Database","url":"https://fds.duke.edu/db/aas/Linguistics/faculty/michael.tomasello/publications/351818"},{"title":"Philosophy Faculty Database","url":"https://fds.duke.edu/db/aas/Philosophy/faculty/michael.tomasello/publications/351818"},{"title":"DEV Warneken20070240 RR S1","url":"https://supp.apa.org/psycarticles/supplemental/a0013860/DEV-Warneken20070240-RR-S1.pdf"},{"title":"Learning Objectives","url":"https://courses.lumenlearning.com/waymaker-psychology/chapter/putting-it-together-motivation-and-emotion/"},{"title":"sources.mandala.library.virginia.edu","url":"https://sources.mandala.library.virginia.edu/node/48471"},{"title":"Extrinsic Rewards Undermine Altruistic Tendencies in 20-Month-Olds","url":"https://mandala-sources-dev.internal.lib.virginia.edu/source/extrinsic-rewards-undermine-altruistic-tendencies-20-month-olds"}]

The paper is Warneken and Tomasello (2008), published in *Developmental Psychology* 44(6), pp. 1785–1788 (doi:10.1037/a0013860). The abstract is the main result I found; I didn't retrieve the full text.

- **Design:** Toddlers who got a material reward in a treatment phase later helped less in a test phase. Toddlers given social praise or nothing helped more. After 20-month-old infants received a material reward during a treatment phase, they subsequently were less likely to engage in further helping during a test phase as compared with infants who had previously received social praise or no reward at all.
- **Interpretation:** The authors describe this as an overjustification effect. They argue that early helping is intrinsically motivated and that rewarding it can erode that motivation. This so-called overjustification effect suggests that even the earliest helping behaviors of young children are intrinsically motivated and that socialization practices involving extrinsic rewards can undermine this tendency.
- **Test tasks:** The supplementary material describes the helping situations. In each, an experimenter dropped an object, such as a marker or clothespin, and feigned difficulty reaching it. The supplement also describes a "jingle machine" used as the reward during treatment.

Links: [{"title":"Examining the Effects of Birth Order on Personality","url":"https://www.diw.de/de/diw_01.c.779086.de/s_9349.html"},{"title":"idw – Informationsdienst Wissenschaft","url":"https://idw-online.de/de/news639781"},{"title":"Firstborn, middle child, or last-born: Birth order has only very small effects on personality","url":"https://innovations-report.com/studies-and-analyses/firstborn-middle-child-or-last-born-birth-order-has-only-very-small-effects-on-personality"},{"title":"Firstborn, middle child, or last-born: Birth order has only very small effects on personality","url":"https://innovations-report.com/?p=275218"},{"title":"Birth order has only very small effects on personality","url":"https://press.uni-mainz.de/?p=19669"},{"title":"Birth order has only very small effects on personality","url":"https://press.uni-mainz.de/birth-order-has-only-very-small-effects-on-personality/"},{"title":"Birth Order Has Little Effect on Narrow Personality Traits","url":"https://psychologicalscience.org/?p=138770"},{"title":"Birth Order Has Little Effect on Narrow Personality Traits","url":"https://www.psychologicalscience.org/publications/observer/obsonline/birth-order-has-little-effect-on-narrow-personality-traits.html"},{"title":"nlsinfo.org","url":"https://nlsinfo.org/bibliography/search/author=Rohrer%2C+Julia+M."}]

The search confirms the core of the 2015 PNAS study (Rohrer, Egloff & Schmukle), but it does not support the "1.5 IQ points" figure.

- **Sample:** The study used three national panels, with about 5,240 Americans, 4,489 Britons and 10,457 Germans. We used data from three large national panels from the United States (n = 5,240), Great Britain (n = 4,489), and Germany (n = 10,457) to resolve this open research question.
- **Personality:** The authors found no birth-order effects on the broad traits. Their abstract lists extraversion, emotional stability, agreeableness, conscientiousness and imagination, and concludes that birth order has no lasting effect on broad traits outside the intellectual domain (Most important, however, we consistently found no birth-order effects on extraversion, emotional stability, agreeableness, conscientiousness, or imagination. On the basis of the high statistical power and the consistent results across samples and analytical designs, we must conclude that birth order does not have a lasting effect on broad personality traits outside of the intellectual domain.)
- **Intellect:** The only personality-adjacent effect was self-reported intellect. The abstract describes a significant decline of a 10th of a SD in self-reported intellect with increasing birth-order position, and this effect persisted after controlling for objectively measured intelligence. Press coverage notes that the study confirmed the already known small decline in objectively measured intelligence from first- to last-born.
- **Magnitude:** The authors themselves called the objective effect tiny. One quote reads that it "is barely meaningful on the individual level, because it is extremely small."

**Unsupported claim:** Nothing I found gives a 1.5-point IQ difference for firstborns. The reported effects are in standard-deviation units (a tenth of an SD for self-reported intellect) and are described only as small. If you need the exact objective IQ or SD figure, check the full PNAS paper (DOI 10.1073/pnas.1506451112), since it wasn't in these results.

**Follow-up:** A later study by Rohrer and colleagues in *Psychological Science* (2018, specification-curve analysis) found no birth-order associations with narrower traits either, though I haven't verified its details here.
