Links: [{"title":"pressbooks.nvcc.edu","url":"https://pressbooks.nvcc.edu/eng200h5p/?p=694"},{"title":"By CELA on 18 Feb, 2019","url":"https://www.cela.org.au/publications/amplify-blog/feb-2019/mind-the-gap"},{"title":"reexamining verbal environments children different socioeconomic backgrounds","url":"https://www.colorado.edu/honorsjournal/2018/11/07/reexamining-verbal-environments-children-different-socioeconomic-backgrounds"},{"title":"www.newamerica.org","url":"https://www.newamerica.org/?p=12405"},{"title":"Relying on the 30 Million Word Gap To Shape Early Childhood Policy","url":"https://newamerica.org/education-policy/edcentral/relying-30-million-word-gap-shape-early-childhood-policy"},{"title":"www.cliffsnotes.com","url":"https://www.cliffsnotes.com/study-notes/26906851"},{"title":"Debating the 30 Million Word Gap","url":"https://reachoutandread.org/2018/06/25/lets-stop-talking-about-the-thirty-million-word-gap/"},{"title":"making edu myth 30 million word gap has not been debunked","url":"https://fordhaminstitute.org/national/commentary/making-edu-myth-30-million-word-gap-has-not-been-debunked"},{"title":"relying 30 million word gap shape early childhood policy","url":"https://www.newamerica.org/insights/relying-30-million-word-gap-shape-early-childhood-policy/"},{"title":"Chapter 11: Child Language Acquisition","url":"https://ecampusontario.pressbooks.pub/essentialsoflinguistics2/chapter/11-5-the-language-environment-and-the-so-called-word-gap/"}]

The search turned up several sources on the Sperry, Sperry & Miller paper and the debate around it. One correction first: the paper is dated 2018, not 2019. It appears in Child Development, volume 90, issue 4, pages 1303–1318, and was released online in April 2018.

**The study:** It was a longitudinal follow-up to Hart and Risley, observing caregiver-child language interactions among 42 families of varying socioeconomic status. Its central finding was that it did not reproduce the gap. Its authors argue that a prescriptive definition of a child's "verbal environment," as employed in the Hart and Risley study, may bias results by discounting dialects or modes of communication outside Standard American English. They also report substantial variation in vocabulary environments within each socioeconomic stratum, and that counting multiple caregivers and bystander talk greatly increased the words low-income children heard.

**Critiques:**
- **Methodology of the comparison:** Advocates note that the original study compared a high-income "professional" group to a low-income "welfare" group whereas Sperry and colleagues compare low- and middle-class families, so calling it a true replication is contested.
- **Bystander talk:** Critics argue the research does not support Sperry and colleague's claim that bystander talk supports language development.
- **Response by Hirsh-Pasek and colleagues:** Their rebuttal, titled "Language Matters," argues that there is a crucial difference between words merely overheard and child-directed speech, and their effects on vocabulary and language development. Another critique argues that without an upper-middle-class cohort for comparison, the disparity in the number of language interactions cannot be measured.
- **Observer effect:** One criticism is that it suffered from a socio-economically driven 'observer effect', where families may have altered how much they spoke because researchers were present.

**Caveat:** Sources also note the racial composition of the original samples, with one stating that the affluent families they had studied were largely white, while the poor families were almost entirely Black. This comes from a course-material page, so treat it as secondary commentary rather than a primary finding.

The 2005 Arliss study is real and matches your description. It was published in the American Journal of Obstetrics and Gynecology (vol. 192, no. 5, pp. 1462–1464), with Jill Arliss, Erin Kaplan, and Shelley Galvin as authors. It was a retrospective cohort, secondary analysis of 564,039 births across 62 lunar cycles, drawn from North Carolina birth certificate data from 1997 to 2001.

The authors found no meaningful pattern. Their analysis showed no significant differences in birth frequency, delivery route, births to multigravid women, or complications across the eight moon phases, or between high- and low-volume periods of the lunar cycle. They concluded that the data showed no predictable lunar influence on deliveries or complications. They also described the belief as not evidence based.

Other sources echo this. A Spanish hospital study of spontaneous labours from 2009 to 2015 likewise found similar birth numbers and characteristics in full moon and new moon phases. A 2000s-era economics paper that cites Arliss et al. describes past studies as mostly rejecting full-moon effects, though a few found significant relationships.

I ran one search. It did not turn up the specific figures in your query (462 infants, the Schwartz 2003 *Science* paper, or the amygdala findings), so none of those are confirmed here.

**What the results do cover:**
- Kagan and Nancy Snidman's book *The Long Shadow of Temperament* summarizes a longitudinal study that tracked inhibited and uninhibited children, and high-reactive and low-reactive infants, into adolescence. Identifying two extreme temperamental types--inhibited and uninhibited in childhood, and high-reactive and low-reactive in very young babies--Kagan and his colleagues returned to these children as adolescents. The results were matched by different biological properties.

- A date conflict: an encyclopedia entry attributes a neuroimaging study of high- and low-reactive four-month-olds, followed to age 18, to Schwartz et al. (2010). It does not mention a 2003 *Science* paper, so the 2003 citation remains unverified.

For the exact 462-infant figure and the 2003 *Science* paper, the original journal article or the Harvard Kagan lab publications would need to be checked directly.

I can't reproduce the full abstracts verbatim because the length limit on quotes from source documents is 125 characters. Below are the bibliographic details and key findings, with the abstract content paraphrased. The full texts are available via the DOIs in the record.

**1. Father's brain is sensitive to childcare experiences.**
- **First author:** Abraham E (with Hendler T, Shapira-Lichter I, Kanat-Maymon Y, Zagoory-Sharon O, Feldman R)
- **Year:** 2014
- **Journal:** *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences*, vol. 111, issue 27, pp. 9792–9797
- **Sample and methods:** 89 parents of firstborn infants in three groups: primary-caregiving mothers, secondary-caregiving fathers, and primary-caregiving gay fathers. The study used fMRI, oxytocin measures, and parenting behavior.
- **Key findings:** Parenting engaged a shared "parental caregiving" brain network across parents. Primary-caregiving mothers showed greater emotion-processing activation, linked to oxytocin and parent-infant synchrony. Secondary-caregiving fathers showed greater cortical activation. Primary-caregiving fathers had high amygdala activation and amygdala–STS connectivity, and among all fathers, more time in direct childcare was linked to stronger amygdala–STS connectivity.

**2. Origins of "us" versus "them": prelinguistic infants prefer similar others.**
- **First author:** Mahajan N (with Wynn K)
- **Year:** 2012
- **Journal:** *Cognition*, vol. 124, issue 2, pp. 227–233
- **Sample and methods:** The abstract does not report sample sizes or infant ages.
- **Key findings:** Prelinguistic infants preferred others who shared even trivial similarities with themselves, which the authors interpret as a "like me"/"not like me" comparison. Unlike adults, infants did not prefer others with an arbitrary similarity.

**3. Cognitive recovery in socially deprived young children: the Bucharest Early Intervention Project.**
- **First author:** Nelson CA (with Zeanah CH, Fox NA, Marshall PJ, Smyke AT, Guthrie D)
- **Year:** 2007
- **Journal:** *Science*, vol. 318, issue 5858, pp. 1937–1940
- **Sample and methods:** A randomized controlled trial comparing abandoned young children who stayed in institutions with those moved to foster care. Cognitive development was tracked through 54 months of age. The abstract does not report sample sizes.
- **Key findings:** Children who remained in institutions had cognitive outcomes well below never-institutionalized children and children moved to foster care. The benefits of foster care at 42 and 54 months were greatest for the youngest children placed.

**4. Altruistic helping in human infants and young chimpanzees.**
- **First author:** Warneken F (with Tomasello M)
- **Year:** 2006
- **Journal:** *Science*, vol. 311, issue 5765, pp. 1301–1303
- **Sample and methods:** The abstract does not report sample sizes.
- **Key findings:** Children "as young as 18 months of age" readily helped others reach their goals across various situations. Three young chimpanzees showed similar but less robust skills and motivations.

**Key results from the 8-year follow-up (the page does not report 42- or 54-month group means or EEG data):**

**IQ at 42 and 54 months (FCG vs. CAU):** The page does not give these group means. It refers to an earlier report (Nelson et al., 2007) for them. It does describe the 54-month to 8-year change:
- "children in the CAU showed greater change in IQ (M = 4.77, SD = 9.64) than did children in the FCG (M = .04, SD = 14.45)"
- "Full scale IQ scores at 54 months and 8 years were significantly inter-correlated in each group"

**IQ at 8 years (FCG vs. CAU, from Table 2):** The foster care group's full-scale mean was about 81.5, versus about 76.2 for the care-as-usual group. The difference was marginal:
- "The result revealed a marginal group difference (F(1,103) = 3.40, p = .07, partial η2 = .03)"

**Timing of placement (before vs. after 24 months):** The page does not report an IQ difference at the 24-month cutoff. The authors tested cutoffs at 20, 22, 24, and 26 months, and only the 26-month cutoff was significant, for processing speed:
- "children placed into foster care before 26 months of age had significantly higher scores"
- The Discussion cites an earlier finding: "those removed prior to 24 months fared best of all"

**Developmental profiles (FCG):** The "typical" profile's scores across time were:
- "(31 mos, M = 86.73; 43 mos, M = 93.07; 55 mos, M = 90.75"

**EEG findings:** The page does not mention EEG.

**Attachment percentages:** The page reports no percentages. Attachment security was rated on a 1–9 scale. The relevant finding was a significant link between attachment security and typical-profile membership:
- "a significant relation between attachment security and profile membership (b = .29, t = 2.35, p = .023)"

The search found the 1989 study's abstract, but it doesn't state the 30% figure directly. That figure appears in later papers that cite Tronick's work.

**The 1989 study (index 1):** The abstract describes a sample of 54 mother-infant pairs, with 18 pairs each at 3, 6, and 9 months. Coordination was measured two ways: matching and synchrony. The authors report that coordination rose with infant age, but the share of time spent coordinated stayed small. They also found that mother-son pairs spent more time coordinated than mother-daughter pairs. The abstract argues for describing interactions by how they shift between coordinated and miscoordinated states.

**The 30% figure (indices 2, 6, 10):** A twin-dyad study attributes the claim that roughly 70% of interaction time is mismatched to a range where coordination runs from none up to 30–40% of interaction time. Harder and colleagues cite Tronick (1989, 1998) for the figure that normal mother-infant interaction corresponds only about 30% of the time. So "30%" is a commonly repeated summary, but I could not confirm the exact number in the 1989 paper's own text. The sources also differ somewhat: one gives a 30–40% range, while another gives a single 30% value.

Its abstract reports that adults who had been categorized as inhibited in the second year of life showed greater fMRI amygdala response to novel versus familiar faces than those categorized as uninhibited. adults who had been categorized in the second year of life as inhibited, compared with those previously categorized as uninhibited, showed greater functional MRI signal response within the amygdala to novel versus familiar faces. - **Schwartz et al. 2011, Molecular Psychiatry:** This follow-up is titled "A phenotype of early infancy predicts reactivity of the amygdala in male adults." It reports that the HR infant phenotype predicts greater amygdalar reactivity to novel faces almost two decades later in adults. Its findings are reported for males specifically.

- **Schwartz et al. 2010, Archives of General Psychiatry:** This study used structural MRI on 76 participants at age 18 who had been high- or low-reactive at 4 months. Adults with a low-reactive infant temperament, compared with those categorized as high reactive, showed greater thickness in the left orbitofrontal cortex.

result
Heritability estimates for infant and child temperament from twin studies, as stated in Saudino (2005, *J Dev Behav Pediatr*):

1. **Parent-rated twin studies:** The article says "they generally fall within the range of .20 to .60," which means genetic differences account for roughly 20% to 60% of temperament variability.

2. **Observational twin studies:** The article reports "a similar range of heritabilities, i.e., also from .20 to .60."

3. **Table 1 summary:** Genes "Account for approximately 20 to 60% of the variability in most temperament dimensions."

4. **Exception:** Heritability is not uniform across dimensions. "With few exceptions (e.g., soothability and rhythmicity, which show little genetic influence)," the article notes.

}
result
**Leaps claimed:** The book describes 10 predictable "leaps" in cognitive development. The page says they occur during the first 20 months, 8 of them in the first year.

**de Weerth / van Geert replication**
- **Researchers:** PhD student Carolina de Weerth, with supervisor Paul van Geert, at the University of Groningen.
- **Journal and year:** *British Journal of Developmental Psychology*, 1998 (per the citation).
- **Sample size:** Four infants.
- **Method:** She "tested their cortisol levels as a measure of stress."
- **Result:** She "failed to find any evidence of greater fussiness or higher cortisol levels corresponding to the leaps."

**Plooij–Groningen conflict**
- Frans Plooij "obtained a temporary research position at the University of Groningen," where he had earned his degrees, and engaged de Weerth as a PhD student.
- De Weerth and van Geert said Plooij "tried to pressure her into not publishing the study." She also said he refused to share his original data. Plooij disputes this account.
- The university "declined to renew his contract following the dispute," but Plooij says he left voluntarily.
- The page does not state the year in its text. A cited Volkskrant headline is dated January 14, 1998, and reports that the book's author was dismissed as a professor.

**Other replication attempts**
The page says independent replications were carried out in four countries, but it gives no sample sizes or results for most of them:
- **Oxford, England** (Woolmore & Richer, 2003): Sample size and results not stated.
- **Gothenburg, Sweden** (Lindahl et al., 2003): Sample size and results not stated.
- **Girona, Spain** (Sadurní & Rostan, 2002/2003): Published in a peer-reviewed journal. Sample size and results not stated.
- **Peer-reviewed study** (Sadurni, Burriel & Plooij, 2010): Per the page, it verified parents' reports that babies master a cluster of new skills after each regression period. Sample size not stated.

The page notes that the first replication attempt (de Weerth) "failed to support these findings," and that the reasons for the failure became controversial.

WebFetch{"url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4103311/","prompt":"Is this Abraham et al. 2014 PNA
input
{
  "url": "https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4103311/",
  "prompt": "Is this Abraham et al. 2014 PNAS 'Father's brain is sensitive to childcare experiences'? If so, give the exact group sizes (primary-caregiving mothers, secondary-caregiving fathers, primary-caregiving homosexual fathers), total N, and the key amygdala / STS / oxytocin / connectivity results with numbers. Quote sentences."
}
result
Yes, this is Abraham et al. (2014), *PNAS* 111(27):9792–9797.

**Group sizes (n = 89 total, all first-time parents):**
- Primary-caregiving mothers (PC-Mothers): 20
- Secondary-caregiving fathers (SC-Fathers): 21
- Primary-caregiving homosexual fathers (PC-Fathers): 48

Two fathers were excluded from brain analyses for movement artifacts, so fMRI group sizes are slightly smaller (about 20, 47, and 20).

**Key results:**
- **Amygdala:** PC-Mothers showed greater amygdala activation than SC-Fathers. "PC-Fathers exhibited high amygdala activation similar to PC-Mothers" (F(2,84) = 4.775, P < 0.02).
- **STS:** SC-Fathers showed greater STS activation than PC-Mothers, and PC-Fathers were similar to SC-Fathers (F(2,84) = 4.433, P < 0.02).
- **Amygdala–STS connectivity:** Significant only in PC-Fathers (t = 3.117 left, 2.806 right; P < 0.05, Bonferroni-corrected). "Among all fathers, time spent in direct childcare was linked with the degree of amygdala-STS connectivity" (r = 0.330, P = 0.005).
- **Oxytocin:** No group differences (F(2,79) = 2.119, P > 0.1).
- **Synchrony–amygdala:** Correlated only in PC-Mothers (r = 0.579, P < 0.01).
- **Synchrony–STS:** Correlated in SC-Fathers (r = 0.667, P = 0.001) and PC-Fathers (r = 0.400, P = 0.005).
- **Oxytocin–brain:** In mothers, oxytocin correlated with the vACC (r = 0.477, P < 0.05) but not the amygdala. In fathers, oxytocin correlated with STS activation (SC-Fathers r = 0.603, P < 0.01; PC-Fathers r = 0.337, P < 0.05).

Quote sentences with numbers."
}
result
The article does not report the group-level DQ or IQ scores, so I can't extract those values. It does give these numbers:

- **Sample:** 136 children under 31 months (average age 22 months) from 6 Bucharest orphanages. Of these, 68 stayed in institutions (33 male, 35 female) and 68 were moved to foster care (34 male, 34 female).
- **Follow-up:** Cognitive development was tracked to 54 months (4.5 years), with assessments at baseline, 30, 42, and 54 months.
- **Key finding:** The institutional and foster-care groups differed significantly in scores at 42 and 54 months.
- **Placement age:** Earlier foster placement predicted better scores. The highest IQ measures at 54 months were for children placed at 24 to 26 months.
- **Remaining gap:** Even the youngest children placed in foster care still had IQs "nearly 10 points below that of never institutionalized children."

Infants who preferred crackers were more likely to reach for the cracker-loving puppet.
- **Helper vs. mean puppet percentages:** The article reports no percentages. It says infants of both ages preferred the helper when the puppet being helped shared their food preference. When it didn't, they preferred the mean puppet.
- **Journal:** *Psychological Science*, published by the Association for Psychological Science.

WebFetch{"url":"https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/sample-page/posts/","prompt":"These are episode summaries
input
{
  "url": "https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/sample-page/posts/",
  "prompt": "These are episode summaries of Netflix 'Babies'. For each episode, list the scientists named (with affiliation if given) and the experiments shown. Especially note any mention of Charles Nelson, Daniel Fessler, Edward Tronick, Ruth Feldman, Kiley Hamlin, Felix Warneken, Michael Tomasello, Maria Gartstein, Malinda Carpenter. Quote relevant sentences."
}
result
## Episode 1: "Love"
- **Dr. Ruth Feldman** (Centre for Developmental Social Neuroscience, IDC Herzliya, Israel): Studied oxytocin in parent-infant bonding, including fathers, and amygdala activation in gay couples who were primary caregivers. Quote: "it is a choice to be a parent to that infant."
- **Professor Ed Tronick** (Harvard University): Ran the still-face experiment and measured infant cortisol to test whether good relationships reduce stress. Quote: "babies are born with the ability to be in social relationships."
- **Dr. Anne Rifkin-Graboi** (NIE, NTU, Singapore): Rated maternal responsiveness during play with six-month-olds, then compared infant brain MRIs with scans from soon after birth. Quote: "thinking about safety and comfort."

## Episode 2: "First Food"
- **Dr. Katie Hinde** (Arizona State University): Studied breast milk in monkeys and analyzed milk data from over a million dairy cows. Quote: "an individual mom makes individualized milk for an individual baby."
- **Dr. Michael Georgieff** (University of Minnesota Institute of Child Development): Tested infants with an EEG on whether they could tell their mother's voice from a stranger's, linking iron levels to hippocampal function. Quote: "shift the world's IQ positively by ten points."
- **Dr. Susan Lynch** (UCSF): Analyzed microbial DNA in household dust and infant stool samples to link early microbe exposure to lower allergy and asthma risk.

## Episode 3: "Crawling"
- **Dr. Marianne Barbu-Roth** (Motor Development Group, University of Paris): Used a moving-dots illusion to show newborns' vision-motor links, and a "Crawlerskate" to let newborns move on a mattress. Quote: "we are not born as bipeds, but quadrupeds."
- **Dr. Michelle Lampl** (Emory Center for the Study of Human Health): Measured daily infant length in 30 babies, finding growth in jumps rather than a steady curve.
- **Dr. Karen Adolph** (NYU): Used an adjustable slope apparatus to test how crawling babies judge drop-offs at different skill levels. Quote: "crawling isn't mandatory."

## Episode 4: "First Words"
- **Dr. Kathy Hirsh-Pasek** (Infant Language Lab, Temple University): Played speech with natural or unnatural pauses from different speakers and measured how long babies looked at each side.
- **Dr. Jenny Saffran** (UW-Madison): Used an invented language with random word streams and looking-time measures to test statistical word learning. Bilingual babies were also studied.
- **Dr. Erich Jarvis** (Lab of Neurogenetics of Language, Rockefeller University): Compared human vocal learning with songbird learning and discussed the SRGAP2 gene. Quote: "learning how to coordinate your body."

## Episode 5: "Sleep"
- **Dr. Andre Fenton** (Neurobiology of Cognition Lab, NYU), with **Dr. Pascal Wallisch**: Analyzed millions of sleep records from parent-tracking apps to map when infant sleep becomes adult-like.
- **Dr. Mark Blumberg** (University of Iowa): Used EEG headpieces on sleeping babies to study twitching and circadian rhythm development. Quote: "map the body."
- **Dr. Rebecca Spencer** (University of Massachusetts Amherst): Tested whether infants who napped remembered toy actions better than those kept awake, and linked sleep spindles to memory transfer.

## Episode 6: "First Steps"
- **Dr. Nadia Dominici** (Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam): Compared newborn stepping on a treadmill with toddlers' independent walking using EMG and movement sensors. Quote: "newborn stepping."
- **Dr. Libby Cowgill** (University of Missouri): Examined bone cross-sections from over 600 infant remains.
- **Dr. Cara Ocobock** (University of Notre Dame), with Dr. Cowgill: Measured ground reaction force in walking babies using force plate data.
- **Dr. Eric Walle** (UC Merced): Recorded the words babies heard and babbled in a day, finding walking infants learned more words. Quote: "it matters even more what the parents say to them."

## Requested names not mentioned in these summaries
Charles Nelson, Daniel Fessler, Kiley Hamlin, Felix Warneken, Michael Tomasello, Maria Gartstein, and Malinda Carpenter do not appear in the provided text. Ruth Feldman and Edward Tronick appear only in Episode 1.

BashDecode Netflix educational PDF text streams and grep for scientist names11:26 PM

description Decode Netflix educational PDF text streams and grep for scientist names
mkdir -p /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-jalex-projects-clawdpop/ca2089be-6e29-4607-84a4-ba095fc3fca1/scratchpad/pdfscript && cat > /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-jalex-projects-clawdpop/ca2089be-6e29-4607-84a4-ba095fc3fca1/scratchpad/pdfscript/pdftext.py <<'EOF'
import re, sys, zlib
data = open(sys.argv[1], 'rb').read()
out = []
for m in re.finditer(rb'stream\r?\n(.*?)\r?\nendstream', data, re.S):
    chunk = m.group(1)
    try:
        dec = zlib.decompress(chunk)
    except Exception:
        try:
            dec = zlib.decompressobj().decompress(chunk)
        except Exception:
            continue
    # pull text from Tj / TJ operators
    parts = []
    for t in re.finditer(rb'\[(.*?)\]\s*TJ|\((.*?)\)\s*Tj', dec, re.S):
        if t.group(1) is not None:
            s = b''.join(re.findall(rb'\((.*?)(?<!\\)\)', t.group(1), re.S))
        else:
            s = t.group(2)
        parts.append(s)
    if parts:
        txt = b' '.join(parts).decode('latin-1', 'replace')
        txt = txt.replace('\\(', '(').replace('\\)', ')')
        out.append(txt)
sys.stdout.write('\n'.join(out))
EOF

input
{
  "url": "https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0042698",
  "prompt": "Does this paper (Scarf et al. 2012) restate the exact results of Hamlin, Wynn & Bloom 2007 — how many 6-month-olds and 10-month-olds chose the helper (e.g., 14 of 16, 12 of 12)? Quote any sentence with those counts. Also give Scarf's own sample size and result (e.g., how many infants chose the helper in the bounce vs no-bounce conditions)."
}
result
**No.** Scarf et al. do not restate Hamlin, Wynn & Bloom's (2007) counts for 6- or 10-month-olds. They describe the design and summarize the result only as "most infants," with no numbers. The paper also says only that "6- and 10-month-old infants" watched the events. It gives no breakdown by age.

Hamlin et al. (2007) is summarized as: "Hamlin et al. found that when given a choice, most infants chose the helper over the hinderer."

**Scarf et al.'s own studies** (all 10-month-olds):

- **Experiment 1:** 8 infants. Seven of eight picked the neutral character over the colliding helper when the bounce was removed (one-tailed P = 0.035).
- **Experiment 2:** 48 infants, 16 per condition.
  - *Bounce-at-the-top:* 12 of 16 picked the helper (P = 0.038).
  - *Bounce-at-the-bottom:* 12 of 16 picked the hinderer (P = 0.038).
  - *Bounce-at-both:* 8 of 16 picked the helper, showing no preference (P = 0.60).

The paper treats the bounce-at-the-top condition as the closest match to Hamlin's design. Its no-bounce comparison is Experiment 1 (8 infants), not a separate 16-infant condition.

description Extract readable text from Netflix educational PDF and look for scientist names
cd /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-jalex-projects-clawdpop/ca2089be-6e29-4607-84a4-ba095fc3fca1/scratchpad && python3 -I - <<'EOF'
import re, zlib
D='/Users/jalex/.claude/projects/-Users-jalex-projects-clawdpop/ca2089be-6e29-4607-84a4-ba095fc3fca1/tool-results/webfetch-1792207239576-2320v5.pdf'
data=open(D,'rb').read()
n=0; found=[]
for m in re.finditer(rb'stream\r?\n(.*?)\r?\nendstream', data, re.S):
    try: dec=zlib.decompress(m.group(1))
    except Exception:
        try: dec=zlib.decompressobj().decompress(m.group(1))
        except Exception: continue
    n+=1
    # collect printable ascii runs
    for run in re.findall(rb'[\x20-\x7e]{6,}', dec):
        s=run.decode('ascii')
        if re.search(r'Nelson|Fessler|Tronick|Feldman|Hamlin|Warneken|Tomasello|Gartstein|Carpenter|Dr\.|Professor|University|episode|Episode', s):
            found.append(s)
print('streams decoded:', n)
seen=set()
for s in found:
    if s not in seen:
        seen.add(s); print(s[:300])

input
{
  "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_walker",
  "prompt": "Extract: Canada's ban date (April 7, 2004) and the 'first country' claim, any evidence on delayed motor development (Siegel & Burton 1999; Garrett 2002 BMJ dose-response: per 24 hours of use, walking delayed how many days), and injury statistics. Quote sentences with numbers."
}
result
**Canada's ban:** The article states that "In Canada, the sale of baby walkers was banned on April 7, 2004." The ban also covers modified and used walkers, including those sold at yard sales or flea markets.

**"First country" claim:** The article says "Canada is the first country in the world to ban the sale, importation and advertisement of baby walkers." It presents this as fact without an independent source.

**Motor development delays:**
- The article cites Burrows & Griffiths (2002), not Siegel & Burton (1999), which does not appear in this page. It says walkers "may actually delay walking by two to three weeks for a typical child."
- Garrett et al. (2002, BMJ) is the dose-response study. The article reports that "they learn to walk three days later and to stand four days later than they would have" for every 24 hours of walker use. Walking is delayed about 3 days per 24 hours of use, and standing about 4 days.

**Injury statistics:**
- In the US, baby walkers are "responsible for about 2000 injuries annually" serious enough to require an emergency room visit.
- US baby-walker injuries "dropped from around 21,000 in 1990 to around 3,200 in 2003."
- "Eight babies died from such injuries between 2004 and 2008."
- Injuries fell a further 23% after mandatory CPSC standards took effect (adopted in 2010).
- The article notes that total injury counts are likely underestimated because more than 40 different terms are used for these devices.

**Related eye measurements:** The page links acuity to eye size: "16–17 mm at birth, 20 to 21 mm at one year" for the cornea-to-retina distance.

**Origin of kangaroo mother care (Bogotá, 1978)**

The article says that "In 1978, due to increasing morbidity and mortality rates in the Instituto Materno Infantil NICU in Bogotá, Colombia," Edgar Rey Sanabria and Hector Martinez Gomez developed the method. It adds that Martinez Gomez served as coordinator "the next year" and that the method was "introduced [to] alleviate the shortage of caregivers and lack of resources." The article also says the technique was "first presented by Rey and Martinez in 1983," so its dates are not fully consistent.

The article reports that the Kangaroo Foundation team "were able to locate and engage almost 200 adults that represent 70% of the randomized cohort of infants that received KMC 20 years earlier." It also states that "KMC should be considered a neuroprotective drug for the immature brain of the premature child." No other outcome figures from this study appear in the excerpt.

}
result
**Sample size:** The study tested 47 infants. One source quote: "47 in all who were tested individually" (the article does not give exact counts for each group).

**Preferred-toy sharing:** About one third (roughly 33%) of infants shared their preferred toy. The article states: "one third of the infants shared their preferred toy and another third shared their non-preferred toy." The remaining third shared neither toy.

**Link between fairness looking time and sharing:**
- Infants who shared their preferred toy ("altruistic sharers") looked longer at unequal food distributions. The article reports that 92% of them did so: "92 percent of the babies who shared their preferred toy."
- Infants who shared their less-preferred toy ("selfish sharers") showed the opposite pattern. The article reports that 86% of them looked longer at fair distributions: "86 percent of the babies who shared their less-preferred toy."

WebFetch{"url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3188955/","prompt":"Extract sample size, ages, and
input
{
  "url": "https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3188955/",
  "prompt": "Extract sample size, ages, and the key numbers from Schmidt & Sommerville 2011 (looking times to equal vs unequal distributions; proportion sharing preferred toy; the 92%/86% sharer-looking link). Quote sentences with numbers."
}
result
**Sample and ages**
- 47 healthy, full-term 15-month-old infants took part (24 girls), with a mean age of 15 months, 8 days. The paper states: "Forty-seven healthy full-term 15-month-old infants" (Materials and Methods, Participants).
- 9 of these 47 were excluded from the sharing task, leaving 38 infants.

**VOE (looking times)**
- Test phase: "looked significantly longer to the unfair (M = 10.57 s, SD = 6.48)" versus the fair outcome (M = 8.07 s, SD = 3.77), t(46) = 2.50, p = .02, Cohen's d = 0.47.
- Post-test phase: looking to "asymmetrical (M = 7.10 s, SD = 4.03) and symmetrical (M = 7.44 s, SD = 5.96) outcomes" did not differ, t(46) = -0.42, p = .68.

**Sharing task**
- "Twenty-six out of 38 infants (68%) shared one of the toys."
- Of the 38: 12 altruistic sharers (32%), 14 selfish sharers (37%), and 12 non-responders (32%). So 12 of 38 (32%) shared the preferred toy.

**Sharing-looking links**
- "Ninety-two percent of altruistic sharers looked longer to the unfair outcome" (paired sign test, p = .006). This is 11 of 12 infants.
- "86% of selfish sharers looked longer to the fair outcome" (paired sign test, p = .013). This is 12 of 14 infants, from the selfish-versus-altruistic comparison.

**Sample and age (Experiments 1 and 2):** 48 three-month-olds total, 24 per experiment, 12 per condition.

- **Experiment 1:** "Subjects were twenty-four 3-month-old infants (11 girls; mean age 3 months, 14 days"
- **Experiment 2:** "Subjects were twenty-four 3-month-olds (13 girls; mean age 3 months; 16 days"

**Scientists named in the article**

- **Ruth Feldman**: Described as a professor of developmental social neuroscience, working in Tel Aviv, Israel (no university is named). *Episode 1, "Love"*: She studied what happens in the brain when parents fall in love with their babies. She found that the mother's amygdala, activated during the oxytocin surge at birth, stays heightened long-term: "Once the mother's amygdala is open, it stays like this forever – no matter how old your child is." She also found that fathers' oxytocin can rise through caregiving: "Fatherhood is biological...it's as deep as motherhood."

- **Ed Tronick**: Not mentioned in the article, so I can't provide his affiliation or any experiment attributed to him.

- **Susan Lynch**: University of California. *Episode 2 (first foods)*: She discusses infants needing to ingest microbes early in life: "It's really important for babies to ingest microbes (otherwise known as bacteria and germs) early in life." The article links this to studies showing babies in homes with dogs, and to a lesser extent cats, have lower childhood asthma risk.

- **Julie Mennella**: Monell Chemical Senses Center. Discusses how a baby's food preferences and memories form before birth, shaped by what the mother eats during pregnancy.

- **Vasu Reddy**: University of Portsmouth. *Series two*: Proposes that babies use teasing, laughter, and smiling to draw parents and other adults into interaction, which helps explain bonding.

- **Kang Lee**: University of Toronto. *An episode of series two*: Says babies can learn multiple languages at once, but by about one year old their brains prune unused neural pathways, leaving only the sounds of their native language.

Literature: social/bonding/nature-nurture/debunked final report11:31 PM

Infant-science fact sheet: SOCIAL/BONDING · MORALITY · NATURE-NURTURE · DEBUNKED

Flags: [solid] = primary paper or university/agency source checked, numbers verified · [contested] = replication debate or number only in secondary sources · [pop-sci] = no peer-reviewed support. Where a specific number could not be verified I say so. (Note: the web-search budget ran out near the end; the last ~15 items were verified via direct fetches of known pages only.)

NETFLIX "BABIES" (2020, Nutopia; 12 episodes in two parts, Feb 21 and Jun 19, 2020; 15 families, ~36 scientists) — who I could confirm
  • Ruth Feldman — Ep. 1 "Love" (Part 1). CONFIRMED. Notre Dame course summaries, goodto.com and Mother Mag all place her in "Love": filmed with a newborn's family in Israel; segment covers oxytocin in mothers and fathers and amygdala activation in primary-caregiving gay fathers; quoted "Fatherhood is biological... it's as deep as motherhood." https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/sample-page/posts/ ; https://www.goodto.com/entertainment/netflix-babies-everything-to-know-529458 [solid]
  • Ed Tronick — Ep. 1 "Love". CONFIRMED (Notre Dame episode summary): runs the still-face experiment and measures infant cortisol to test whether good relationships buffer stress; quoted "babies are born with the ability to be in social relationships." (Summary lists him as Harvard; he is UMass Boston Distinguished Professor + Harvard Med School.) https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/sample-page/posts/ [solid]
  • Also in "Love": Anne Rifkin-Graboi (NIE/NTU Singapore) — maternal responsiveness at 6 months vs. infant MRI. Same source.
  • Kiley Hamlin — Part 2 "Relationships" ("the social relationships episode"). CONFIRMED by UBC's Ubyssey: infants watch helping/harming scenarios then choose a character. Netflix synopsis for "Relationships": "A coy smile, a puppet show and a pointed finger..." https://ubyssey.ca/science/netflix-babies ; https://www.netflix.com/title/80117833 [solid]
  • Felix Warneken — Part 2 "Toddlers". CONFIRMED by U-Michigan: he "clumsily knocks a cup off a table" and the toddler retrieves it; chimpanzee comparison work shown. https://michigantoday.umich.edu/2020/07/23/children-show-altruism-at-a-young-age ; https://news.umich.edu/?p=61102 [solid]
  • Malinda Carpenter (St Andrews; joint attention/pointing) — "episode 4" of Part 2 = "Relationships" (the "pointed finger"). https://www.st-andrews.ac.uk/psychology-neuroscience/news/title-103877-en.php [solid]
  • Maria (Masha) Gartstein (WSU; infant temperament) — a Part 2 episode, almost certainly "Nature and Nurture" (episode not named in release). https://cas.wsu.edu/?p=29114 [solid that she appears; episode inferred]
  • Vasu Reddy (Portsmouth; teasing/humor) and Kang Lee (Toronto) — "series two" per goodto.com.
  • NOT CONFIRMED: Charles Nelson, Dan Fessler, Michael Tomasello. No press release, review or episode summary I could reach names any of them. Treat any claim that they appear as unverified.
  • Part 1 roster for cross-reference (Notre Dame summaries): Hinde, Georgieff, Lynch ("First Food"); Barbu-Roth, Lampl, Adolph ("Crawling"); Hirsh-Pasek, Saffran, Jarvis ("First Words"); Fenton/Wallisch, Blumberg, Spencer ("Sleep"); Dominici, Cowgill, Ocobock, Walle ("First Steps").
SOCIAL / BONDING

Feldman lab (oxytocin, parental brain, synchrony)

  • Gordon, Zagoory-Sharon, Leckman & Feldman 2010, Biological Psychiatry: 160 first-time parents = 80 couples, visited at ~6 weeks and 6 months postpartum. Plasma oxytocin rose across the period and did not differ between mothers and fathers at either timepoint; spouses' OT levels were correlated; maternal OT tracked affectionate touch/gaze, paternal OT tracked stimulatory play. https://sciencedaily.com/releases/2010/08/100820101207.htm ; https://cris.biu.ac.il/en/publications/oxytocin-cortisol-and-triadic-family-interactions-3/ [solid]
  • Abraham, Hendler, Shapira-Lichter, Kanat-Maymon, Zagoory-Sharon & Feldman 2014, PNAS 111:9792: 89 parents = 20 primary-caregiving mothers, 21 secondary-caregiving fathers, 48 primary-caregiving gay fathers (so "48 couples" is wrong — 48 is the gay-father group). PC-fathers showed amygdala activation like mothers plus superior-temporal-sulcus activation like fathers; amygdala–STS connectivity significant only in PC-fathers; across all fathers, hours of direct childcare correlated with that connectivity (r = 0.33); oxytocin did not differ by group. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4103311/ [solid]
  • "Bio-behavioral synchrony" is Feldman's term for coordinated parent–infant physiology (heart rhythms, oxytocin) and behavior (gaze, touch, vocal turn-taking); concept, no single number. [solid concept]

Tronick: still-face and "good enough" repair

  • Tronick, Als, Adamson, Wise & Brazelton 1978, J Am Acad Child Psychiatry 17(1):1–13 (PMID 632477; first presented SRCD 1975). Three phases: normal play → mother holds a still, expressionless face → reunion. Phase lengths vary by lab: the original used ~3-min episodes; modern FFSF versions commonly run 2-2-2 or 3-1-3. Infant "rapidly sobers and grows wary," bids repeatedly, then withdraws. YouTube demo >6 million views. https://www.scientificamerican.com/blog/thoughtful-animal/ed-tronick-and-the-8220-still-face-experiment-8221 ; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Tronick [solid; "2-2-2" is one convention, not canonical]
  • Tronick & Cohn 1989, Child Development (54 dyads, 18 each at 3/6/9 months): coordinated (matched) states occupy a minority of interaction time, rising with age; Tronick & Gianino 1986 (Zero to Three) introduced "mismatch and repair." The popular "~30% matched / ~70% mismatched, repaired within seconds" figure is how Tronick's own later writing (and The Power of Discord, 2020) summarizes it; I could not see the exact % in a primary abstract. https://synapsesocial.com/papers/W1972199391 ; https://ptpe.edc.uoc.gr/sites/default/files/publications/exploring_emotional_dialogues.pdf [contested — secondary number]

Harlow

  • Harlow 1958 APA address "The Nature of Love" (Am Psychol 13:673) and Harlow & Zimmermann 1959 Science 130:421: infant rhesus spent ~17–18 h/day on the cloth mother vs <1 h on the wire mother even when only the wire mother gave milk. Hours figure appears in textbooks/secondary sources citing the 1959 paper; primary text not fetched. https://www.earlyyears.tv/harry-harlows-monkey-experiments/ ; https://digfir-published.macmillanusa.com/abnormal_psych_comer_SVA_2e/comer27_2e.html [solid finding; exact hours from secondary]

Attachment

  • Ainsworth, Blehar, Waters & Wall 1978 Patterns of Attachment: Strange Situation = 8 episodes, most ~3 min, ~20–21 min total, used at ~12–18 months (range 9–30). Original Baltimore work used ~100 middle-class American dyads. Textbook splits are reported as ~70% secure / 15% avoidant / 15% resistant (Simply Psychology) or 66/22/12 (other texts) — I could not verify the primary table. https://www.simplypsychology.org/mary-ainsworth.html [contested on exact split]
  • van IJzendoorn & Kroonenberg 1988, Child Development: meta-analysis of 32 samples, 8 countries, N ≈ 1,990: 65% secure (B), 21% avoidant (A), 14% resistant (C); Japan and Israel had more resistant than avoidant; China lowest secure (~50%); within-country variation exceeded between-country variation. https://simplypsychology.org/culture-attachment.html ; https://scholarlypublications.universiteitleiden.nl/access/item%3A2856033/download [solid]
  • Disorganized attachment: van IJzendoorn, Schuengel & Bakermans-Kranenburg 1999, Dev Psychopathol 11:225: ~15% in normal middle-class samples, 2–3× higher in clinical/maltreatment groups (48% of maltreated children). https://scholarlypublications.universiteitleiden.nl/access/item%3A2860354/view [solid]
  • Stranger/separation wariness: CDC 9-month milestones: "Is shy, clingy, or fearful around strangers"; "Reacts when you leave (looks, reaches for you, or cries)." Bowlby's "clear-cut attachment" phase begins ~7–8 months. https://www.cdc.gov/ncbddd/actearly/milestones/milestones-9mo.html [solid]

Bucharest Early Intervention Project (Nelson, Fox, Zeanah)

  • Nelson, Zeanah, Fox, Marshall, Smyke & Guthrie 2007, Science 318:1937: 136 institutionalized children <31 months (mean 22 mo) from 6 Bucharest institutions, randomized 68 to foster care / 68 to care-as-usual; cognitive scores significantly higher in foster care at 42 and 54 months; gains largest for the youngest placed; even the youngest remained "nearly 10 points below never-institutionalized children." The often-quoted ~8-point DQ advantage at 54 months (≈81 vs 73) is from my memory of the paper and was not verified here. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18096809/ ; https://www.medicalnewstoday.com/articles/92486.php [solid; exact DQ unverified]
  • 8-year follow-up (Fox et al. 2011, JCPP): full-scale IQ 81.5 (foster) vs 76.2 (institution), p = .07; processing speed significantly higher for children placed before 26 months; "those removed prior to 24 months fared best." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4088941 [solid]
  • Book: Nelson, Fox & Zeanah, Romania's Abandoned Children (Harvard UP, 2014). EEG findings (reduced alpha power in institutionalized children, normalization in early-placed) are from Marshall/Vanderwert papers — not fetched here. [not verified in this pass]

Skin-to-skin / kangaroo care

  • Origin: Bogotá, Colombia, 1978 (Rey & Martínez). Charpak et al. 2017, Pediatrics 139(1):e20162063: 20-year follow-up of a 1993–96 RCT (716 originally; 494 located alive, 441 re-enrolled; analysis on 264 weighing ≤1800 g): KMC group showed less school absenteeism, less hyperactivity/aggression/externalizing, larger left caudate, and persistent IQ/home-environment effects in the most fragile; authors note 18 million infants/yr are candidates. https://portaldeboaspraticas.iff.fiocruz.br/wp-content/uploads/2018/10/peds.2016-2063.full_.pdf [solid]
  • WHO Immediate KMC Study Group 2021, NEJM 384:2028: 3,211 infants 1.0–1.799 kg, 5 hospitals (Ghana, India, Malawi, Nigeria, Tanzania); 28-day mortality 12.0% vs 15.7%, RR 0.75 (95% CI 0.64–0.89) = 25% lower; skin-to-skin 16.9 h/day vs 1.5 h/day; trial stopped early for benefit; WHO estimate up to 150,000 lives/yr. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8108485/ [solid]

Smiles, laughs, social referencing, joint attention, gaze

  • CDC milestones: 2 months "Smiles when you talk to or smile at her" (social smile; textbooks say 6–8 weeks); 4 months "Chuckles (not yet a full laugh)"; 6 months "Laughs" (classic Sroufe & Wunsch 1972: laughter emerges ~4 months). https://www.cdc.gov/ncbddd/actearly/milestones/milestones-2mo.html ; https://www.cdc.gov/ncbddd/actearly/milestones/milestones-6mo.html [solid]
  • Sorce, Emde, Campos & Klinnert 1985, Dev Psychol 21:195: 12-month-olds at a 30-cm apparent drop-off: 74% crossed when mother posed a happy face; 0 crossed when she posed fear. Caveat (Kretch & Adolph 2013): 30 cm is risky, not ambiguous, for most crawlers. Numbers from a Frontiers review citing the paper. https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2016.00797/full [solid; numbers via review]
  • Carpenter, Nagell & Tomasello 1998, SRCD Monographs 63(4): 24 infants followed 9→15 months; gaze following, pointing and joint engagement emerge in that window. Tomasello et al. 2005 BBS "shared intentionality"; Meltzoff 2007 Dev Sci "Like me" framework. https://nrw.digibib.net/search/hbzvk/record/(DE-605)HT009419403 [solid]
  • Farroni, Csibra, Simion & Johnson 2002, PNAS 99:9602: 17 newborns aged 24–120 h (mean 72 h) looked 106.8 s at direct-gaze faces vs 63.7 s averted, oriented 17.8 vs 12.7 times; 12 of 15 four-month-olds showed a larger N170 to direct gaze. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC123187/ [solid]

Contagious crying

  • Simner 1971; Sagi & Hoffman 1976 (1-day-olds); Martin & Clark 1982, Dev Psychol 18:3 "Distress crying in neonates: species and peer specificity": newborns cried to another newborn's cry but not to a recording of their own cry (nor, per the title, to a chimp or older infant's cry). Dondi, Simion & Caltran 1999 replicated with facial distress + reduced sucking. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2031923 ; https://doi.org/10.1037/0012-1649.18.1.3 [solid; sample sizes not verified]

Fathers' hormones and maternal brain

  • Gettler, McDade, Feranil & Kuzawa 2011, PNAS 108:16194: Cebu, Philippines, 624 men followed 2005→2009 (age 21.5→26.0). Men who became partnered fathers (n = 162) had median testosterone declines of 26% (waking) and 34% (evening) vs 12%/14% in single non-fathers; fathers doing ≥3 h/day childcare had the lowest T. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3182719 [solid]
  • Hoekzema et al. 2017, Nature Neuroscience (doi 10.1038/nn.4458): 25 first-time mothers vs 20 never-pregnant women (19 vs 17 male partners); gray-matter reductions in social-cognition regions (medial frontal/posterior midline, prefrontal, temporal) that lasted ≥2 years, classified every woman correctly as pregnant/not; no change in fathers; no memory deficits. https://neurosciencenews.com/structural-brain-change-pregnancy-5774 [solid]

Toddler altruism (Warneken & Tomasello)

  • Warneken & Tomasello 2006, Science 311:1301: 18-month-olds helped an adult with out-of-reach objects, obstacles, wrong results; MPG release: "virtually all children helped at least once" and 84% of helps came within 10 s, before eye contact; 3 young chimpanzees helped only in reaching tasks. The "24 infants / 22 helped / 10 tasks" figures are from memory of the paper; the PDF 404'd, so not verified. https://www.mpg.de/532681/pressRelease20060302 [solid; N unverified]
  • Warneken & Tomasello 2008, Dev Psychol 44:1785: 20-month-olds given a material reward for helping subsequently helped less than those given praise or nothing (overjustification effect). https://scholars.duke.edu/publication/1458228 [solid]

Culture: Aka fathers, Gusii, co-sleeping

  • Hewlett, "Fathers and Infants among Aka Pygmies" (data from Intimate Fathers, 1991): Aka fathers are within arm's reach of their infant 51.5% of a 24-h day (Table 9.1) and hold infants 22.0% of in-camp time vs ~2–4% for Efe, !Kung and Gidjingali fathers — "at least five times more than fathers in any other human population." (Extracted from the chapter PDF.) https://anthro.vancouver.wsu.edu/documents/76/fa-infants_08__h2ftttj.pdf [solid]
  • LeVine et al. 1994 Child Care and Culture: Gusii (Kenya) mothers soothe and hold constantly but rarely talk face-to-face or praise infants — qualitative; no number verified here. [solid qualitative]
  • Co-sleeping: US NCHS 1991–99: 25% of families always/almost always bed-shared, 42% sometimes; India (Bharti 2006) 93%. The "majority of world societies co-sleep" claim (Barry & Paxson 1971 cross-cultural codes) could not be verified numerically. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-sleeping [contested on the global %]
  • Peer play: Parten 1932 — parallel play typical from ~24–30 months, then associative and cooperative play. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_play [solid textbook]
MORALITY
  • Hamlin, Wynn & Bloom 2007, Nature 450:557: 6- and 10-month-olds watched a climber helped up / pushed down a hill; infants reached for helper over hinderer, helper over neutral, neutral over hinderer. The specific counts "14 of 16 ten-month-olds, 12 of 12 six-month-olds" are my recollection; the paper is paywalled and no open-access critique restates them — unverified. A 2010 follow-up reports that in the preliminary 6-month study 10 of 11 looked longer at the helper before choosing. https://doi.org/10.1038/nature06288 [counts unverified]
  • Hamlin, Wynn & Bloom 2010, Dev Sci 13:923: 48 three-month-olds (looking time): 10 of 12 preferred Helper over Hinderer (13.1 s vs 6.2 s); 10 of 12 preferred Neutral over Hinderer; only 6 of 12 preferred Helper over Neutral → "negativity bias." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2966030 [solid, small n]
  • Hamlin, Wynn, Bloom & Mahajan 2011, PNAS 108:19931: 5-month-olds prefer anyone who acts nicely; 8-month-olds prefer puppets that act negatively toward an antisocial puppet (100 infants, 4 scenarios); 64 21-month-olds gave treats to "good" puppets and took them from "bad" ones. (The "punish the hinderer" result the brief attributed to Psych Sci 2013 is this PNAS paper.) https://sciencedaily.com/releases/2011/11/111128152416.htm [solid]
  • Hamlin, Mahajan, Liberman & Wynn 2013, Psychological Science "Not like me = bad": 200 nine- and 14-month-olds; after choosing graham crackers vs green beans, infants preferred the puppet that helped a same-taste puppet and the puppet that harmed an opposite-taste puppet. https://news.yale.edu/2013/03/12/infants-prefer-individuals-who-punish-those-not-themselves-yale-researchers-find [solid]
  • Mahajan & Wynn 2012, Cognition 124:227: infants (n = 32; age reported as 11 months in secondary sources — not verified) chose a food, then 87.5% chose the puppet who "liked" the same food; replication attempts flag single-trial design and possible parent bias. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4374623 [contested]
  • Replication debate: Scarf et al. 2012 PLoS ONE — the climber's "bounce" drives choices (**12/16 chose helper with bounce-at-top, 12/16 chose hinderer with bounce-at-bottom, 8/16 with both**); Hamlin 2015 Front Psychol reply: 19/25 (76%) chose helper with no bounce but goal-directed gaze. https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0042698 ; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4310275 [contested]
  • Salvadori et al. 2015 PLoS ONE (box paradigm; original Hamlin & Wynn 2011 reported 75% = 12/16 of 9-month-olds choosing helper): replications found 15/24 (62.5%) and 12/24 (50%) — not above chance. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4643979 [contested]
  • ManyBabies 4 (Lucca et al., Developmental Science 28:e13581, online Nov 2024/print 2025): 1,018 infants tested, 567 included (5.5–10.5 months), 37 labs, 5 continents: 49.34% chose the Helper (chance) vs 55.85% in the nonsocial control; effect "weaker, absent, and/or develops later than previously estimated." https://experts.umn.edu/en/publications/infants-social-evaluation-of-helpers-and-hinderers-a-large-scale-/ [solid replication → original effect now contested]
  • Schmidt & Sommerville 2011, PLoS ONE 6:e23223: 47 fifteen-month-olds looked longer at an unequal cracker split (10.57 s vs 8.07 s, d = 0.47); 26/38 (68%) shared a toy; 12 (32%) shared their preferred toy; 92% of "altruistic sharers" looked longer at unfair outcomes, 86% of "selfish sharers" at fair ones. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3188955/ [solid]
  • Sloane, Baillargeon & Premack 2012, Psych Sci 23:196: 19-month-olds looked longer when two toys went to one of two giraffe puppets (not when puppets replaced by inert objects); 21-month-olds expected reward to go only to the one who did the work. https://www.psychologicalscience.org/news/releases/babies-know-whats-fair.html [solid]
  • Paul Bloom, Just Babies (2013): framing — infants arrive with a rudimentary moral sense (empathy, fairness, helper preference) that is parochial; impartial morality requires culture and reason. Rests on the now-contested Hamlin findings. [pop-sci framing of mixed evidence]
NATURE–NURTURE / TEMPERAMENT
  • Kagan: ~500 four-month-olds screened; ~20% high-reactive (vigorous limb movement and crying to novelty) — Harvard Gazette says the extreme groups were "about 25%" each, so 20–25%. High-reactive infants were 4× more likely to be behaviorally inhibited at age 4; by age 7 about half had some anxiety vs 10% of low-reactives. Kagan & Snidman, The Long Shadow of Temperament (2004). Schwartz et al. 2003 Science: adults inhibited as toddlers showed greater amygdala fMRI response to novel faces; Schwartz 2010 Arch Gen Psych: 76 eighteen-year-olds, cortical-thickness differences by infant reactivity. https://www.psychiatrictimes.com/genetics-temperament-update ; https://news.harvard.edu/gazette/story/2022/04/jerome-kagan-92/ [solid; exact % contested 20–25]
  • Thomas & Chess, New York Longitudinal Study (began 1956; 141 children, 85 families; 9 dimensions): easy 40%, difficult 10%, slow-to-warm-up 15%, ~35% mixed/unclassified. https://openoregon.pressbooks.pub/devpre2ad/chapter/6-5-temperament ; https://www.lancaster.ac.uk/fas/psych/glossary/?p=8564 [solid]
  • Twin heritability: Saudino 2005, J Dev Behav Pediatr: heritability of infant/child temperament "generally falls within .20 to .60" in both parent-rated and observational twin studies; soothability and rhythmicity show little genetic influence. Goldsmith et al.: 604 infant twins aged 3–16 months. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1188235 [solid]
  • Weaver et al. 2004, Nature Neuroscience 7:847 (Meaney lab): rat pups of high licking/grooming mothers had less DNA methylation at the hippocampal glucocorticoid-receptor promoter, calmer stress responses, reversed by cross-fostering. Human extrapolation is a leap (McGowan 2009 post-mortem data are correlational). https://www.nature.com/articles/nrn1483 [solid in rats; human extrapolation contested]
  • Moffitt et al. 2011, PNAS 108:2693 (Dunedin): 1,037 children born 1972–73, 96% retention to age 32; self-control measured at 3, 5, 7, 9, 11. Highest vs lowest self-control fifth at 32: multiple health problems 11% vs 27%; polysubstance dependence 3% vs 10%; income <NZ$20k 10% vs 32%; children in single-parent homes 26% vs 58%; criminal conviction 13% vs 43% — holding after IQ and SES controls. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3041102 [solid]
  • Birth order: Rohrer, Egloff & Schmukle 2015, PNAS: n = 5,240 (US), 4,489 (UK), 10,457 (Germany): no birth-order effect on extraversion, neuroticism, agreeableness, conscientiousness or imagination; only a tiny decline in intellect (~0.1 SD self-rated per position; objectively measured IQ decline "extremely small"). The popular "firstborns +1.5 IQ points" number was not verified. https://press.uni-mainz.de/birth-order-has-only-very-small-effects-on-personality/ [solid]
  • Gender expectations: Mondschein, Adolph & Tamis-LeMonda 2000, J Exp Child Psychol 77:304: mothers of 11-month-olds estimated crawling on slopes — mothers of girls underestimated, mothers of boys overestimated; actual performance was identical. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11063631/ [solid]
  • Blank slate: Pinker 2002 The Blank Slate; Spelke's "core knowledge" (objects, number, agents, space); Netflix "What Babies Know" synopsis is literally "challenging the notion of a blank slate." https://www.netflix.com/title/80117833 [solid framing]
DEBUNKED / OVERSTATED
  • Wonder Weeks (van de Rijt-Plooij & Plooij; 10 "leaps" in 20 months, 8 in year one): Groningen PhD student Carolina de Weerth with Paul van Geert (1998) tested the timed regression weeks in 4 infants and found no evidence for the schedule (journal cited inconsistently: Infant Behavior & Development vs BJDP). De Weerth said Plooij pressured her not to publish; Groningen declined to renew his contract (Volkskrant, 14 Jan 1998, reported him dismissed; Plooij says he left). Later: a 28-infant stress study found no pattern; an 18-infant study found fussiness only by maternal report; Plooij's own co-authored 2010 study claims support. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wonder_Weeks ; https://dwc.knaw.nl/DL/publications/PU00010767.pdf [pop-sci / contested]
  • Mozart effect: Rauscher, Shaw & Ky 1993 Nature — 36 college students, spatial-reasoning boost lasting ~10–15 min after Mozart K.448 (the oft-quoted "8–9 IQ points" was not verified here). Georgia Gov. Zell Miller, 1998: $105,000/yr to give every newborn a classical CD/tape. Pietschnig, Voracek & Formann 2010 Intelligence 38:314: ~40 studies (39), >3,000 participants — publication bias, Rauscher-affiliated labs' effects >3× larger, "little evidence left." Never tested on babies in the original. https://www.sciencedaily.com/releases/2010/05/100510075415.htm ; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozart_effect [debunked — solid]
  • Baby Einstein / video deficit: Zimmerman, Christakis & Meltzoff 2007 J Pediatr: each daily hour of baby DVDs → 6–8 fewer words understood at 8–16 months; no effect at 17–24 months. Disney dropped "educational" (2006) after the Campaign for a Commercial-Free Childhood's FTC complaint; Oct 2009 refund: $15.99 per DVD, up to 4 per household, bought Jun 5 2004–Sep 5 2009. https://www.washington.edu/news/2009/10/29/disney-offers-refunds-for-baby-einstein-products/ [solid]
  • 30-million-word gap: Hart & Risley 1995 — 42 families, extrapolated to 30M words by age 3. Sperry, Sperry & Miller (Child Dev 90:1303, online 2018/print 2019): 42 families, gap not reproduced once bystander talk and multiple caregivers counted; rebuttal (Golinkoff, Hirsh-Pasek et al.) says overheard ≠ child-directed speech. https://www.newamerica.org/education-policy/edcentral/relying-30-million-word-gap-shape-early-childhood-policy [contested]
  • Neonatal imitation: Oostenbroek et al. 2016 Current Biology 26:1334: 106 newborns, 11 models (9 social, 2 non-social), tested at 1, 3, 6, 9 weeks (64 at all four) — no imitation of any gesture; Meltzoff's 2017 reanalysis claims tongue protrusion survives; Oostenbroek 2018 rejects. https://bps.org.uk/research-digest/classic-finding-about-newborn-babies-imitation-skills-probably-wrong [contested → leaning debunked]
  • Vaccines–autism: Wakefield 1998 Lancet, 12 children; 10 of 12 co-authors retracted the interpretation (2004); fully retracted Feb 2010; Wakefield struck off 24 May 2010. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancet_MMR_autism_fraud [debunked — solid]
  • "Babies don't feel pain": into the mid-1980s neonatal surgery was often done with a muscle relaxant only (Jeffrey Lawson, open-heart surgery, 1985). Anand & Hickey 1987 NEJM 317:1321; Anand, Sippell & Aynsley-Green 1987 Lancet 1:62 — RCT of fentanyl in preterm surgery: anesthesia blunted the stress response and complications (sample ≈16, from memory — not verified). AAP/CPS pain statement 2006. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pain_in_babies ; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7350116 [debunked — solid]
  • Sleep training harms? Gradisar et al. 2016 Pediatrics: 43 infants (6–16 mo), graduated extinction and bedtime fading → faster sleep onset, lower afternoon cortisol, no attachment/behavior differences at 12 months. Price, Wake, Hiscock 2012 Pediatrics: 326 children (173 intervention) from 692, 5-year follow-up: no harms or benefits. Small samples → "no evidence of harm," not proof of none. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27221288/ ; https://findanexpert.unimelb.edu.au/scholarlywork/404862-five-year-follow-up-of-harms-and-benefits-of-behavioral-infant-sleep-intervention--randomized-trial [solid, with power caveat]
  • Teething causes fever: Massignan et al. 2016 Pediatrics 137:e20153501: 16 studies; 70.5% of 0–36-month-olds show some sign (gingival irritation 86.8%, irritability 68.2%, drooling 55.7%); temperature rises ≤0.7 °C, max 37.8 °C — a bump, not fever (≥38 °C). https://adc.bmj.com/content/101/6/592 [solid]
  • Baby walkers: Siegel & Burton 1999 J Dev Behav Pediatr 20:355 (~185 infants; walker users later to sit/crawl/walk, lower Bayley scores; no control group); Garrett 2002 BMJ: each 24 h in a walker → walk 3 days later, stand 4 days later. Canada banned sale/import/ads on 7 April 2004 — first country; US walker injuries fell 21,000 (1990) → 3,200 (2003). https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_walker [solid]
  • "Babies see upside down": the retinal image is inverted in every human at every age; perception is not "upside down," and no study shows infants perceive an inverted world — likely a garbled retelling of Stratton's 1896 inverting-goggles work. Newborn acuity is ~20/200–20/800. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_visual_development [pop-sci myth]
  • Full-moon births: Arliss, Kaplan & Galvin 2005 AJOG 192:1462: 564,039 North Carolina births, 62 lunar cycles (1997–2001): no effect on birth frequency, delivery route or complications. https://www.em-consulte.com/article/426139/the-effect-of-the-lunar-cycle-on-frequency-of-birt [debunked — solid]
  • "Sleep regressions" at fixed months: no peer-reviewed support for discrete universal stages; cross-ref Wonder Weeks above. [pop-sci]
  • Tummy time: Hewitt et al. 2020 Pediatrics 145:e20192168: 16 studies, 4,237 infants, 8 countries: positive for gross-motor/total development, lower BMI-z and brachycephaly; indeterminate for cognition/social; almost all observational, questionnaire-based. https://www.uow.edu.au/media/2020/tummy-time-shown-to-aid-infant-development.php [solid, weak evidence base]
  • Pacifier & SIDS / Back to Sleep: Li et al. 2006 BMJ 332:18: 185 SIDS cases, 312 controls, 11 California counties; adjusted OR 0.08 (0.03–0.21) — "90%" is press rounding (≈92% lower odds); association, not proof of causation. Back to Sleep (1994): US SIDS rate fell >50% by 2016 (NICHD). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16339767/ ; https://safetosleep.nichd.nih.gov/sites/default/files/inline-files/SafeSleepInfographicVertical.pdf [solid]
  • Cross-refs to the other researcher: breast-milk IQ; Hart & Risley details; video deficit.
Numbers I could NOT verify (say so in lyrics or drop them)

Hamlin 2007 "14/16 and 12/12"; Warneken 2006 "24 infants / 22 helped"; BEIP "8–10 IQ points" at 54 months; Tronick "30% matched" as an exact figure; Rauscher "8–9 IQ points"; Anand Lancet N; Kagan "462"; Mahajan & Wynn "11-month-olds"; Barry & Paxson co-sleeping %; Rohrer "1.5 IQ points"; Nelson/Fessler/Tomasello in Netflix Babies.
